Importance-based eviction; all $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-7}$.}\n\\label{tab:seg-robust}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & Tercile & Quartile\\; [95\\% CI] & Quintile\\; [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{DeepSeek} & 0.15 & 0.391 & 0.491\\; $[0.44,\\; 0.70]$ & 0.505\\; $[0.33,\\; 0.71]$ \\\\\n& 0.20 & 1.215 & 1.388\\; $[1.05,\\; 1.79]$ & 1.197\\; $[1.02,\\; 1.76]$ \\\\\n& 0.25 & 3.986 & 4.322\\; $[3.62,\\; 8.06]$ & 4.960\\; $[3.80,\\; 9.02]$ \\\\\n& 0.30 & 8.481 & 12.930\\; $[9.83,\\; 19.10]$ & 11.548\\; $[10.06,\\; 18.51]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen} & 0.15 & 0.066 & 0.226\\; $[0.14,\\; 0.27]$ & 0.203\\; $[0.13,\\; 0.26]$ \\\\\n& 0.20 & 0.307 & 0.561\\; $[0.35,\\; 0.77]$ & 0.517\\; $[0.29,\\; 0.75]$ \\\\\n& 0.25 & 1.074 & 1.870\\; $[1.05,\\; 2.44]$ & 1.801\\; $[1.13,\\; 2.38]$ \\\\\n& 0.30 & 3.155 & 4.097\\; $[2.91,\\; 4.94]$ & 4.009\\; $[3.28,\\; 5.34]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe quartile and quintile $\\Delta$ values are comparable to or slightly larger than tercile values because finer-grained segmentation isolates more extreme phases (the ``lowest'' quintile captures a more concentrated low-sparsity segment than the lowest tercile). The monotonic dose-response ($\\Delta$ increasing with $s$) is preserved across all three segmentation schemes for both models, confirming that the severity gradient is not an artifact of the tercile segmentation choice.\n\n\n\\section{Robustness to Asymmetric Sparsity Allocation}\n\\label{app:asymmetric-robustness}\n\nOur main experiments use symmetric sparsity ($s_{\\text{head}} = s_{\\text{KV}} = s$). To verify that the phase-gated severity gradient does not depend on this symmetric assumption, we evaluate two asymmetric configurations at a fixed total sparsity budget equivalent to $s{=}0.25$, using importance-based eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Asymmetric sparsity robustness: per-phase II under two head/KV allocations (DeepSeek, importance-based eviction, $n{=}50$ spot check).}\n\\label{tab:asymmetric-robust}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nConfig & $s_{\\text{head}}$ & $s_{\\text{KV}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nA (KV-heavy) & 0.20 & 0.30 & 19.51 & 6.05 & 0.38 & $+$19.13 \\\\\nB (head-heavy) & 0.30 & 0.20 & 18.77 & 3.97 & 0.52 & $+$18.25 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSymmetric & 0.25 & 0.25 & 3.97 & --- & 0.02 & $+$3.99 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nBoth asymmetric configurations produce large positive $\\Delta$ ($+19.13$ and $+18.25$), confirming that the phase-gated severity gradient is robust to the head/KV allocation ratio. The KV-heavy configuration (A) exhibits higher mid-phase II ($6.05$ vs.\\ $3.97$), suggesting that the KV eviction axis has a stronger impact on intermediate reasoning phases. Both asymmetric $\\Delta$ values exceed the symmetric baseline ($3.99$), consistent with one axis operating at $s{=}0.30$---above the symmetric maximum tested---which amplifies overall severity. A systematic investigation of the full asymmetric interaction surface is left for future work.\n\n\n\\section{Outlier Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{app:outlier-robustness}\n\nThe extreme mean/median ratio (up to $97{,}640{:}1$ in individual conditions) reflects heavy-tailed II distributions driven by near-zero denominators in specific problems. We verify robustness via symmetric winsorization at $5\\%$ and $10\\%$ levels. All median-based statistics---paired median difference $\\Delta$, Wilcoxon signed-rank $p$-values, and amplification ratio $G$---are strictly invariant under winsorization, as expected for rank-based estimators. The five core claims---compound exceeds individual, importance exceeds random, monotonic dose-response, $G \\gg 1$ for importance, $G \\approx 1$ for random---are fully robust. The extreme outlier severity further validates our choice of median-based inference over mean-based alternatives. $G$ values in this robustness analysis follow the ratio-of-medians convention (Table~\\ref{tab:profiling-subset}); the primary $G$ definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) is similarly invariant as a median of per-problem ratios.\n\n\n\\section{\\texttt{&lt;think>} Token Phase Validation}\n\\label{app:think-validation}\n\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B generates explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>}...\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiters that demarcate chain-of-thought reasoning from final answers, providing a model-generated ground truth for reasoning phase boundaries. We evaluate whether our attention-sparsity-based phase segmentation (\\S\\ref{par:phase-segmentation}) recovers these boundaries on GSM8K ($n{=}48$ problems with valid phase assignments), using adaptive per-sequence quantile segmentation rather than a fixed global threshold.\n\n\\paragraph{Naming convention.}\nA note on terminology: DeepSeek's \\texttt{&lt;think>} phase (active reasoning) exhibits \\emph{low} attention sparsity---attention is broadly distributed across many positions during exploratory computation. Conversely, the answer phase exhibits \\emph{high} attention sparsity---attention concentrates on a few positions during structured output. Thus PACS ``low-sparsity phase'' corresponds to the model's thinking phase, and ``high-sparsity phase'' to the answer phase. The physical phenomenon is consistent: broadly distributed attention characterizes reasoning in both frameworks.\n\n\\paragraph{Token-level agreement.}\nWe binarize each token as ``thinking'' (inside \\texttt{&lt;think>}) or ``answer'' (outside) and compare against the sparsity-based phase assignment. The adaptive segmentation achieves substantial agreement: $\\kappa{=}0.88$ (per-problem mean $0.85$, median $0.88$), with token-level accuracy of $94.2\\%$. Per-problem AUC-ROC is $0.98$ (median $0.99$), indicating near-perfect discriminability between thinking and answer phases based on attention sparsity alone. Of problems, $75\\%$ achieve $\\kappa \\geq 0.80$ and $100\\%$ achieve $\\kappa \\geq 0.60$. The \\texttt{&lt;think>} phase exhibits significantly lower attention sparsity than the answer phase ($0.656$ vs.\\ $0.763$; Cohen's $d{=}-0.80$), confirming that attention distribution breadth reliably distinguishes reasoning from structured computation.\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary proximity.}\nThe median distance between the sparsity-based phase boundary and the nearest \\texttt{&lt;think>}/\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiter is 1 token, with $81.2\\%$ of boundaries falling within 10 tokens. This tight alignment indicates that the attention sparsity transition closely tracks the model's self-identified shift from exploratory reasoning to structured answer generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase composition and II correlation.}\nWe examine whether the fraction of thinking tokens within each phase correlates with compound severity (II). At the mid-phase level, the thinking-token fraction correlates with II (Spearman $\\rho{=}0.49$, $p < 0.001$), consistent with mid-phase segments that contain more reasoning content being more vulnerable to compound sparsity. Low- and high-phase correlations are non-significant, as expected: these phases are compositionally dominated by thinking or answer tokens, respectively, leaving insufficient variance for correlation.\n\n\\paragraph{MATH-500 indirect validation.}\nDirect $\\kappa$ validation requires explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, available only on GSM8K. On MATH-500 ($n{=}500$), we instead assess \\emph{temporal ordering consistency}: the fraction of problems where the low-sparsity phase precedes the high-sparsity phase, consistent with a thinking-then-answering generation structure. DeepSeek achieves a temporal ordering score of $0.576$ with $100\\%$ of problems exhibiting the expected direction (low-sparsity before high-sparsity). Qwen, which lacks \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, shows weaker but directionally consistent temporal ordering ($0.479$). This indirect evidence supports the validity of sparsity-based phase segmentation on MATH-500 but does not constitute direct $\\kappa$ validation.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nThe direct $\\kappa$ validation ($\\kappa{=}0.88$, $n{=}48$) is limited to DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B on GSM8K; MATH-500 has only indirect (temporal ordering) validation. Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct does not produce explicit reasoning delimiters, precluding direct $\\kappa$ validation on either benchmark. Phase segmentation is computed on the dense model's attention patterns and is independent of sparsity level, so the agreement statistics are reported once rather than per-$s$.\n\n\n\\section{Amplification Ratio ($G$) Details}\n\\label{app:g-metric}\n\nThe per-problem amplification ratio $G^{(i)} = \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{low}} / \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{high}}$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) provides a complementary ratio perspective to the primary metric $\\Delta$. Table~\\ref{tab:g-metric} reports $G$ with bootstrap 95\\% CIs for all conditions.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-problem amplification ratio $G$ (median of $G^{(i)}$) with bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$). $G \\gg 1$ indicates phase-concentrated severity. Importance-based eviction exhibits strong phase modulation ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ for DeepSeek, $2.6$--$36.9{\\times}$ for Qwen); random eviction remains near $G \\approx 1$.}\n\\label{tab:g-metric}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $G$\\; [95\\% CI] (DeepSeek) & $G$\\; [95\\% CI] (Qwen) \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & $\\mathbf{7.5}$\\; $[4.1,\\; 11.4]$ & n.d.$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\n0.15 & Random     & 0.5\\; $[0.4,\\; 0.6]$ & 0.7\\; $[0.4,\\; 0.8]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & $\\mathbf{17.9}$\\; $[12.7,\\; 26.3]$ & $\\mathbf{2.6}$\\; $[0.5,\\; 5.1]$ \\\\\n0.20 & Random     & 0.8\\; $[0.6,\\; 0.9]$ & 0.9\\; $[0.7,\\; 1.0]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & $\\mathbf{49.1}$\\; $[40.1,\\; 57.8]$ & $\\mathbf{9.5}$\\; $[5.2,\\; 14.8]$ \\\\\n0.25 & Random     & 1.1\\; $[1.0,\\; 1.2]$ & 1.3\\; $[1.2,\\; 1.4]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & $\\mathbf{79.8}$\\; $[63.3,\\; 96.5]$ & $\\mathbf{36.9}$\\; $[25.3,\\; 49.1]$ \\\\\n0.30 & Random     & 1.1\\; $[1.0,\\; 1.3]$ & 1.3\\; $[1.2,\\; 1.4]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.85\\textwidth}}{\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$ $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} \\approx 0$; ratio undefined ($G$ CI: $[-0.2, 0.9]$). $\\Delta{=}0.07$, $p{=}8.7 \\times 10^{-15}$ confirms $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low}} > \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nStrong phase modulation is specific to importance-based eviction ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ for DeepSeek, $2.6$--$36.9{\\times}$ for Qwen). Random eviction shows weak but statistically significant modulation at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($G \\approx 1.1$--$1.3$; CIs exclude 1), while at $s \\leq 0.20$ the CIs span or touch 1. The monotonic increase in $G$ with sparsity parallels the $\\Delta$ dose-response in Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}.\n",

```text
1	\section{Importance Discrimination Collapse Details}
2	\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}
3	
4	This cross-model consistency (Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes importance discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$), confirming that importance discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy.
5	
6	
7	\section{Extended Discussion}
8	\label{app:extended-discussion}
9	
10	\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}
11	The compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \leq 0.30$).
12	
13	\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}
14	At $n{=}500$, random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \approx 0.20$: non-significant at $s \leq 0.20$ for both models, but significant at $s \geq 0.25$ (DS: $\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$; QW: $\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $2.7$--$17{\times}$ weaker than importance-based (restricted to conditions where both strategies are significant, $s \geq 0.25$), confirming that the dominant amplification is strategy-specific.
15	
16	\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}
17	Directly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.
18	
19	
20	\section{Pilot Mitigation Experiment}
21	\label{app:pilot-mitigation}
22	
23	We explored oracle phase-aware budget reallocation, shifting sparsity budget from high-severity low-sparsity phases to low-severity high-sparsity phases ($n{=}30$ problems at $s{=}0.25$). Oracle recovery---the fraction of compound interaction eliminated by phase-aware allocation---reached 18\% (95\% CI: $[-39\%, 45.8\%]$; Wilcoxon $p = 0.002$), below the pre-registered 30\% gate for claiming practical mitigation. A heuristic variant achieved 32\% recovery (95\% CI: $[-22.8\%, 66\%]$; $p = 0.024$). \textbf{Critically, 95\% bootstrap CIs for both conditions span zero}, meaning neither recovery estimate is statistically distinguishable from no improvement. \textbf{Gate status: FAIL}---neither oracle nor heuristic recovery meets the pre-registered threshold, and neither is statistically distinguishable from zero improvement. These results indicate that phase-aware budget reallocation alone is insufficient to neutralize the severity gradient; effective mitigation likely requires joint optimization of budget allocation and eviction strategy selection across phases.
24	
25	
26	\section{Extended Limitations}
27	\label{app:limitations}
28	
29	\paragraph{Model scale.}
30	All experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.
31	Whether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.
32	Larger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the importance discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).
33	
34	
35	\paragraph{Architectural scope.}
36	Our two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.
37	The cross-training-paradigm replication (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.
38	Pilot cross-architecture evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\ref{app:cross-arch}) suggests qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This preliminary evidence ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \leq 0.25$ non-significant) is consistent with the qualitative pattern extending beyond Qwen2 but is insufficient for definitive cross-architecture claims; quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.
39	
40	
41	\paragraph{Task coverage.}
42	We evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.
43	Different reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.
44	Generalizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.
45	
46	
47	\paragraph{Statistical scope.}
48	Bootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (seed$=$42). The primary analysis uses $n{=}496$ paired problems for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded); the permutation test (Table~\ref{tab:permutation}) uses $n{=}49$ from an initial profiling subset.
49	The severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratios up to $43{:}1$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives.
50	All four profiling-subset permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. At $n{=}496$, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm all conditions with $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ (Table~\ref{tab:cohens-d}). The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification.
51	
52	At $n{=}500$, random eviction on both models becomes significant at $s \geq 0.25$ (Qwen: $\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ at $s{=}0.25$; $\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ at $s{=}0.30$; bootstrap CIs exclude zero).
53	These effects are genuine but $2.7$--$20{\times}$ weaker than importance-based eviction at the same sparsity levels, consistent with a weak phase gradient that is detectable at sufficient sample size but not the primary driver of compound severity.
54	
55	\paragraph{Sparsity range.}
56	Our primary analysis spans $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$; pilot data at $s{=}0.10$ and $s{=}0.35$ exist (6 total sparsity levels) but are not included in the main analysis due to limited sample size at these boundary conditions. Monotonicity is established at the tested sparsity levels (JT test $p \ll 10^{-10}$); the functional form of $G(s)$ between and beyond these levels is not characterized.
57	More extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.
58	
59	
60	\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}
61	We do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.
62	
63

All statistics use medians, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests, and bootstrap median CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$), matching the MATH-500 methodology. DeepSeek importance-based eviction replicates the MATH-500 phase gradient across all four sparsity levels (all $p \leq 0.0003$) with a strict dose-response ($\Delta$: $0.24 \to 4.68$). Qwen importance-based eviction is significant at $s \geq 0.25$ ($p < 10^{-4}$). Random eviction is non-significant across all conditions for both models; Qwen random $s{=}0.30$ is excluded due to data quality concerns (output truncation producing extreme II values).

119	\section{Downstream Accuracy Validation Details}

122	Table~\ref{tab:accuracy} reports MATH-500 accuracy under compound sparsity (importance-based eviction) at $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$ with \texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$8192, complementing the PPL-based analysis in \S\ref{sec:accuracy-validation}. Qwen ($n{=}100$; truncation $\leq 4\%$ across all conditions) provides the cleanest dose-response evidence: accuracy declines gradually from $46\%$ (dense) through $s{=}0.25$ ($\delta{=}-4$ to $-13$\,pp) before a sharp cliff at $s{=}0.30$ ($19\%$, $\delta{=}-27$\,pp). DeepSeek ($n{=}50$) shows steeper overall degradation ($74\% \to 4\%$), confounded by rising truncation ($16\% \to 76\%$); non-truncated analysis (\S\ref{app:accuracy-nt}) reveals a dual-regime structure.

124	\begin{table}[h]
125	\centering
126	\small
127	\caption{MATH-500 accuracy under compound sparsity (importance-based eviction, \texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$8192). $\delta$: accuracy change vs.\ dense. Trunc: truncation rate under sparse condition. DS: $n{=}50$ (dense truncation $16\%$); QW: $n{=}100$ (truncation $\leq 4\%$ all conditions).}
128	\label{tab:accuracy}
129	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
130	\toprule
131	Model & $s$ & Dense & Sparse & $\delta$ & Trunc \\
132	\midrule
133	DS & 0.15 & 74\% & 48\% & $-$26\,pp & 32\% \\
134	DS & 0.20 & 74\% & 30\% & $-$44\,pp & 56\% \\
135	DS & 0.25 & 74\% & 8\% & $-$66\,pp & 76\% \\
136	DS & 0.30 & 74\% & 4\% & $-$70\,pp & 76\% \\
137	\addlinespace
138	QW & 0.15 & 46\% & 42\% & $-$4\,pp & $\leq$4\% \\
139	QW & 0.20 & 46\% & 36\% & $-$10\,pp & $\leq$4\% \\
140	QW & 0.25 & 46\% & 33\% & $-$13\,pp & $\leq$4\% \\
141	QW & 0.30 & 46\% & 19\% & $-$27\,pp & $\leq$4\% \\
142	\bottomrule
143	\end{tabular}
144	\end{table}
145	
146	\paragraph{Failure mode divergence.}
147	DeepSeek and Qwen exhibit qualitatively distinct failure modes under compound sparsity. DeepSeek failures are \emph{truncation-dominated}: compound sparsity is associated with verbose, repetitive chain-of-thought that exceeds the token limit (truncation rises from $16\%$ at dense to $32\%$ at $s{=}0.15$ and $76\%$ at $s{=}0.30$, even with \texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$8192). Qwen failures are \emph{wrong-answer-dominated}: outputs complete within the token limit (truncation $\leq 4\%$) but produce incorrect answers, confirming that compound sparsity disrupts reasoning quality directly. This divergence likely reflects training-paradigm differences: DeepSeek's distillation from DeepSeek-R1 produces extended CoT traces susceptible to truncation under degraded conditions.
148	
149	\paragraph{Language switching under high sparsity.}
150	DeepSeek problems exhibit language switching to Chinese mid-generation at the following observed rates: 0\% at dense, 4\% at $s{=}0.15$, and 40\% at $s{=}0.30$ (intermediate sparsity levels not yet measured).\footnote{Language switching rates were detected from \texttt{gen\_snippet} fields in 2048-token evaluations, not from the independent 8192-token generations used in Table~\ref{tab:accuracy}. Rates under the 8192-token budget were not re-measured.} This qualitative pathology---absent in Qwen---is a \emph{symptom} of compound-sparsity-induced degradation rather than a cause of accuracy loss: at $s{=}0.30$, Chinese-language and English-language outputs achieve comparable accuracy ($\sim$10\%), indicating that the accuracy decline reflects pruning-induced reasoning corruption regardless of output language. The switching pattern is consistent with the model's Chinese-language pretraining corpus surfacing under degraded attention in distillation-trained models.
151	
152	
153	\section{Non-Truncated Sensitivity Analysis}
154	\label{app:accuracy-nt}
155	
156	With \texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$8192, Qwen truncation remains $\leq 4\%$ across all sparsity levels, so the full-sample accuracy decline ($\delta{=}-4$ to $-27$\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:accuracy}) reflects pure reasoning quality degradation without truncation confound.
157	
158	DeepSeek exhibits substantial truncation ($16\%$ at dense to $76\%$ at $s{=}0.25$--$0.30$). Restricting to non-truncated outputs reveals a \emph{dual-regime} structure. At $s \leq 0.20$, non-truncated accuracy remains $68$--$71\%$ (vs.\ $88\%$ dense), while full-sample accuracy drops to $30$--$48\%$---indicating that degradation at low sparsity is primarily truncation-driven rather than reflecting reasoning quality loss. At $s \geq 0.25$, non-truncated accuracy itself collapses: $33\%$ at $s{=}0.25$ and $17\%$ at $s{=}0.30$ ($n_{\text{NT}}{=}12$ for both; reported as reference values given small samples), demonstrating that high-dose compound sparsity degrades both generation length \emph{and} reasoning quality simultaneously. The NT accuracy cliff from $68\%$ ($s{=}0.20$) to $33\%$ ($s{=}0.25$) localizes the transition between truncation-driven and dual-collapse regimes.
159	
160	This dual-regime structure complements the importance discrimination collapse analysis (\S\ref{sec:analysis}): at low sparsity, compound interaction primarily manifests as increased verbosity (degraded attention produces repetitive CoT); at high sparsity, the interaction additionally corrupts reasoning-critical computations.
161	
```

      "content": "\\section{Importance Discrimination Collapse Details}\n\\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}\n\nThis cross-model consistency (Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes importance discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$), confirming that importance discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended-discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}\nThe compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \\leq 0.30$).\n\n\\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \\approx 0.20$: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$ for both models, but significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (DS: $\\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$; QW: $\\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $2.7$--$17{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based (restricted to conditions where both strategies are significant, $s \\geq 0.25$), confirming that the dominant amplification is strategy-specific.\n\n\\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}\nDirectly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.\n\n\n\\section{Pilot Mitigation Experiment}\n\\label{app:pilot-mitigation}\n\nWe explored oracle phase-aware budget reallocation, shifting sparsity budget from high-severity low-sparsity phases to low-severity high-sparsity phases ($n{=}30$ problems at $s{=}0.25$). Oracle recovery---the fraction of compound interaction eliminated by phase-aware allocation---reached 18\\% (95\\% CI: $[-39\\%, 45.8\\%]$; Wilcoxon $p = 0.002$), below the pre-registered 30\\% gate for claiming practical mitigation. A heuristic variant achieved 32\\% recovery (95\\% CI: $[-22.8\\%, 66\\%]$; $p = 0.024$). \\textbf{Critically, 95\\% bootstrap CIs for both conditions span zero}, meaning neither recovery estimate is statistically distinguishable from no improvement. \\textbf{Gate status: FAIL}---neither oracle nor heuristic recovery meets the pre-registered threshold, and neither is statistically distinguishable from zero improvement. These results indicate that phase-aware budget reallocation alone is insufficient to neutralize the severity gradient; effective mitigation likely requires joint optimization of budget allocation and eviction strategy selection across phases.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.\nWhether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.\nLarger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the importance discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).\n\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-training-paradigm replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nPilot cross-architecture evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-arch}) suggests qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \\sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This preliminary evidence ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) is consistent with the qualitative pattern extending beyond Qwen2 but is insufficient for definitive cross-architecture claims; quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (seed$=$42). The primary analysis uses $n{=}496$ paired problems for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded); the permutation test (Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}) uses $n{=}49$ from an initial profiling subset.\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratios up to $43{:}1$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives.\nAll four profiling-subset permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. At $n{=}496$, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm all conditions with $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification.\n\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction on both models becomes significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (Qwen: $\\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ at $s{=}0.25$; $\\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ at $s{=}0.30$; bootstrap CIs exclude zero).\nThese effects are genuine but $2.7$--$20{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based eviction at the same sparsity levels, consistent with a weak phase gradient that is detectable at sufficient sample size but not the primary driver of compound severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur primary analysis spans $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$; pilot data at $s{=}0.10$ and $s{=}0.35$ exist (6 total sparsity levels) but are not included in the main analysis due to limited sample size at these boundary conditions. Monotonicity is established at the tested sparsity levels (JT test $p \\ll 10^{-10}$); the functional form of $G(s)$ between and beyond these levels is not characterized.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\n\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n\n\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{app:gsm8k}\n\nTables~\\ref{tab:gsm8k-ds} and \\ref{tab:gsm8k-qw} report the per-phase II breakdown on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) for DeepSeek and Qwen, respectively. All statistics use medians, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests, and bootstrap median CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$), matching the MATH-500 methodology. DeepSeek importance-based eviction replicates the MATH-500 phase gradient across all four sparsity levels (all $p \\leq 0.0003$) with a strict dose-response ($\\Delta$: $0.24 \\to 4.68$). Qwen importance-based eviction is significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($p < 10^{-4}$). Random eviction is non-significant across all conditions for both models; Qwen random $s{=}0.30$ is excluded due to data quality concerns (output truncation producing extreme II values).\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-phase interaction index on GSM8K: DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}50$). $\\Delta$: paired median difference ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) with bootstrap 95\\% CI ($B{=}10{,}000$, median statistic). $p$: Wilcoxon signed-rank, Bonferroni-corrected ($k{=}4$).}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k-ds}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\Delta$\\; [95\\% CI] & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & $-$0.001 & 0.241 & 0.24\\; $[0.08,\\; 0.48]$ & 0.0003 \\\\\n0.15 & Random     & 0.036 & 0.105 & $-$0.04\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.90 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & $-$0.002 & 0.740 & 0.74\\; $[0.31,\\; 1.12]$ & ${<}\\,10^{-4}$ \\\\\n0.20 & Random     & 0.143 & 0.423 & 0.28\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.96 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & 0.009 & 1.992 & 1.99\\; $[0.93,\\; 2.82]$ & ${<}\\,10^{-4}$ \\\\\n0.25 & Random     & 0.619 & 1.603 & 0.98\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.83 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & 0.024 & 4.682 & 4.68\\; $[2.12,\\; 10.56]$ & ${<}\\,10^{-4}$ \\\\\n0.30 & Random     & 1.837 & 4.683 & 2.85\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.54 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-phase interaction index on GSM8K: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n{=}50$). 1	
2	\section{Results}
3	\label{sec:results}
4	
5	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity}
6	\label{sec:strategy-severity}
7	
8	Table~\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference across strategies and sparsity levels. Under importance-based eviction, $\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$---a $22{\times}$ amplification (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \approx 0.20$: at $s \leq 0.20$, $\Delta$ is non-significant ($0.01$--$0.03$; CIs span zero); at $s \geq 0.25$, $\Delta$ becomes significant ($0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$) but $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than under importance-based eviction. Both strategies exhibit monotonic dose-response (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), but importance masking effect sizes are an order of magnitude larger.

26	\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification}
27	\label{sec:phase-amplification}
28	
29	Decomposing II by reasoning phase reveals that strategy-dependent severity is driven by amplification in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\ref{tab:main-results} reports the per-phase breakdown. Under importance-based eviction, the median II in the low-sparsity phase rises from $0.357$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $8.456$ ($s{=}0.30$), while the high-sparsity phase remains near zero ($0.003$--$0.038$), yielding paired median differences $\Delta$ from $0.39$ to $8.48$ (all $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction shows a detectable but much weaker phase gradient at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$), $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than importance-based at matched sparsity. Figure~\ref{fig:hero} visualizes the dose-response relationship for both models. The per-problem amplification ratio $G$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) provides a complementary ratio perspective and is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric}.
30	Under the multiplicative null ($r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$; Appendix~\ref{app:multiplicative-null}), the phase-severity gradient is preserved ($G_{\times}{=}21$--$64{\times}$); we report additive-null values throughout as the more conservative estimates.

77	
78	
79	\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover}
80	\label{sec:crossover}
81	
82	The phase-strategy crossover is model-dependent. For DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at all four sparsity levels: at $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields \emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\ $1.113$) but \emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$), with reversal magnitude ranging from $2.2{\times}$ to $4.0{\times}$ across $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. Qwen does \emph{not} exhibit this crossover: importance-based eviction produces lower II than random in both phases (e.g., at $s{=}0.25$: high $0.004$ vs.\ $1.108$; low $1.053$ vs.\ $1.769$). This divergence suggests the crossover arises from model-specific attention head specialization patterns rather than a universal compound-sparsity property. Full crossover analysis appears in Appendix~\ref{app:crossover-details}.
83	
84	\subsection{Replication Across Training Paradigms}
85	\label{sec:cross-model}
86	
87	DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (distillation-trained) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (instruction-tuned) share the Qwen2 backbone but differ in training objective, providing a within-architecture-family comparison across training paradigms.
88	Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\text{eff}}{=}475$) reproduces the monotonic severity gradient: $\Delta$ increases from $0.07$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 10^{-14}$), paralleling DeepSeek at approximately one-quarter to one-sixth the magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing median $\Delta$ trajectories (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z \geq 6.2$, $p < 10^{-9}$; see \S\ref{par:stats}). Random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \leq 0.20$, becoming significant at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$) but $2.7$--$20{\times}$ weaker than importance-based. A caveat: 45.2\% of Qwen problems yield null mid-phase segments due to low temporal autocorrelation ($r{=}0.718$ vs.\ $0.894$); this does not affect $\Delta$ (Appendix~\ref{app:qwen-null-rate}). Full tables in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-details}.
89	
90	\subsection{Preliminary Cross-Architecture Analysis}
91	\label{sec:cross-arch}
92	
93	Preliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, $n{=}49$)\footnote{The $n{=}49$ sample is an initial profiling subset. Full-scale ($n{=}500$) LlamaForCausalLM replication is planned as future work; the current analysis prioritizes within-architecture replication (Qwen2) over cross-architecture breadth.} provides preliminary support for the phase-gated severity pattern under importance-based eviction (post-hoc power $> 0.99$ for within-importance gradient) but is inconclusive for strategy comparisons (power $0.26$--$0.63$); the interaction magnitude is ${\sim}40{\times}$ smaller than DeepSeek, suggesting that quantitative characteristics may be Qwen2-family-specific and that broader architectural diversity (e.g., Mistral, Gemma) is needed for generalization claims. Full results, including per-sparsity breakdowns and a power analysis motivating the sample size needed for definitive cross-architecture claims, appear in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-arch}.
94	
95	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}
96	\label{sec:cross-benchmark}
97	
98	To assess task generalization, we evaluate both models on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) at $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. On DeepSeek (clean data, no output truncation), importance-based eviction replicates the MATH-500 phase gradient across all four sparsity levels with a strict dose-response: $\Delta{=}0.24$ at $s{=}0.15$ rising to $4.68$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all $p \leq 0.0003$, Wilcoxon). Strategy specificity on GSM8K manifests as a \emph{qualitative} gap: importance-based eviction produces significant phase gradients while random eviction is non-significant across all conditions (CIs spanning zero). We attribute this to \emph{task complexity}: GSM8K's simpler arithmetic is associated with more uniform attention distributions, yielding a flatter sparsity landscape; within MATH-500, longer traces exhibit higher strategy specificity (Spearman $\rho > 0$, $p < 0.02$), supporting cognitive load as the modulating variable. Qwen GSM8K results are inconclusive due to generation truncation (\texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$2047): while directional consistency holds at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}1.14$--$4.62$; $p < 10^{-4}$), absolute values and phase assignments are unreliable because all outputs hit the token limit. Full tables in Appendix~\ref{app:gsm8k}.
99	
100	\paragraph{GSM8K accuracy under compound sparsity.}
101	A power analysis on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) reveals a strategy-dependent asymmetry in downstream accuracy. Under random head removal, all 8 conditions (2 models $\times$ 4 sparsity levels) are non-significant (max $|d|{=}0.164$). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $n{=}50$ can detect effects of $d \geq 0.40$ ($\alpha{=}0.05$, power${=}0.80$); all random conditions showed $|d| \leq 0.164$, meaning the null results may reflect insufficient power rather than true absence of effect. Under importance-based pruning, Qwen exhibits a medium-sized accuracy degradation at $s \geq 0.25$ ($d \approx 0.63$, power ${>}\,0.99$), consistent with the PPL-based severity gradient observed on MATH-500. DeepSeek importance conditions exhibit a qualitative downward trend in accuracy but are excluded from parametric inference due to heavy-tailed distributions that violate test assumptions.
102	
103	\subsection{Statistical Validation}
104	\label{sec:stat-validation}
105	
106	Table~\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports the paired median difference $\Delta$ under importance-based eviction for both models at $n{=}496$/$475$. All eight conditions yield CIs excluding zero; within-importance dose-response $d_z$ (s015 vs.\ s030) is $0.94$ (DeepSeek) and $0.92$ (Qwen), both large effects. Importance-based eviction produces significantly larger phase gradients than random at all four sparsity levels ($p_{\text{Bonf}} \ll 10^{-10}$, Wilcoxon; permutation tests in Appendix~\ref{app:permutation-details}).

122	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\footnotesize All $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$. Within-importance $d_z$ (s015 vs.\ s030): DS $0.94$, QW $0.92$.}
123	\end{tabular}
124	\end{table}
125	
126	\subsection{Answer-Region Analysis}
127	\label{sec:answer-region}
128	
129	Across both models, 93.2\% of answer tokens (\verb|\boxed{}| markers) fall within the high-sparsity phase, where compound interaction is minimal ($G \approx 1$). This non-uniformity identifies \emph{where} severity concentrates: reasoning-trace tokens show $5.7$--$43.5{\times}$ PPL degradation vs.\ $1.3$--$5.3{\times}$ for answer tokens (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). However, reasoning-trace degradation may produce \emph{silent reasoning errors}---correct formatting with corrupted intermediate logic \cite{lanham-2023-faithfulness, turpin-2023-biased-cot}---a failure mode that PPL detects but answer-level accuracy may miss.
130	
131	\subsection{Downstream Accuracy Validation}
132	\label{sec:accuracy-validation}
133	
134	To validate that the PPL-based severity gradient translates to downstream task performance, we evaluate MATH-500 accuracy under compound sparsity (importance-based eviction, \texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$8192; Table~\ref{tab:accuracy}). Qwen's minimal truncation ($\leq 4\%$) provides a clean PPL-accuracy bridge.
135	Qwen ($n{=}100$; truncation $\leq 4\%$) shows gradual decline from $46\%$ (dense) through $s{=}0.25$ ($\delta{=}-4$ to $-13$\,pp) with a sharp cliff at $s{=}0.30$ ($19\%$, $\delta{=}-27$\,pp). DeepSeek ($n{=}50$) degrades more steeply ($74\% \to 4\%$) with a dual-regime structure: at $s \leq 0.20$, degradation is primarily truncation-driven (non-truncated accuracy remains $68$--$71\%$ at $s{=}0.15$--$0.20$ vs.\ $88\%$ dense); at $s \geq 0.25$, both truncation and reasoning quality collapse simultaneously (Appendix~\ref{app:accuracy-details}).
136	The two models exhibit distinct failure modes reflecting training-paradigm dependence: DeepSeek failures are \emph{truncation-dominated} (truncation $16\% \to 76\%$), while Qwen failures are \emph{wrong-answer-dominated} (truncation $\leq 4\%$, reasoning quality degrades directly). Given minimal truncation, Qwen accuracy data provide a reliable PPL-accuracy bridge; DeepSeek accuracy results, confounded by truncation rates of $16\%$--$76\%$, should be interpreted as qualitative observations only.
137	
138	We provide a correlational mechanistic analysis in terms of importance discrimination collapse in \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
139	
```

Full tables in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-details}.\n\n\\subsection{Preliminary Cross-Architecture Analysis}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, $n{=}49$)\\footnote{The $n{=}49$ sample is an initial profiling subset. The task says to add bootstrap 95% CI to the n=50 pilot results. Let me check the c5b_pilot experiment and any other experiments that have pilot CI data.


I'm looking at c9's metrics for bootstrap CI data, but it only contains power analysis values, not the pilot table CIs I need. The importance conditions show the delta values with their 95% CIs already calculated, which is exactly what I need to cite.

The real gap is that the MATH-500 profiling subset table lacks confidence intervals on the delta estimates, though that's less critical since the power analysis itself focuses on GSM8K accuracy rather than the profiling data. I'm searching through the registry data more systematically to see if there's an existing experiment that computed confidence intervals for the n=50 MATH-500 profiling subset, which would let me include them in the table rather than just pointing readers elsewhere.

```text
146	\paragraph{MATH-500 indirect validation.}
147	Direct $\kappa$ validation requires explicit \texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, available only on GSM8K. On MATH-500 ($n{=}500$), we instead assess \emph{temporal ordering consistency}: the fraction of problems where the low-sparsity phase precedes the high-sparsity phase, consistent with a thinking-then-answering generation structure. DeepSeek achieves a temporal ordering score of $0.576$ with $100\%$ of problems exhibiting the expected direction (low-sparsity before high-sparsity). Qwen, which lacks \texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, shows weaker but directionally consistent temporal ordering ($0.479$). This indirect evidence supports the validity of sparsity-based phase segmentation on MATH-500 but does not constitute direct $\kappa$ validation.
148	
149	\paragraph{Scope.}
150	The direct $\kappa$ validation ($\kappa{=}0.88$, $n{=}48$) is limited to DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B on GSM8K; MATH-500 has only indirect (temporal ordering) validation. Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct does not produce explicit reasoning delimiters, precluding direct $\kappa$ validation on either benchmark. Phase segmentation is computed on the dense model's attention patterns and is independent of sparsity level, so the agreement statistics are reported once rather than per-$s$.
151	
152	
153	\section{Amplification Ratio ($G$) Details}
154	\label{app:g-metric}
155	
156	The per-problem amplification ratio $G^{(i)} = \mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{low}} / \mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{high}}$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) provides a complementary ratio perspective to the primary metric $\Delta$. Table~\ref{tab:g-metric} reports $G$ with bootstrap 95\% CIs for all conditions.
157	
158	\begin{table*}[t]
159	\centering
160	\small
161	\caption{Per-problem amplification ratio $G$ (median of $G^{(i)}$) with bootstrap 95\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$). $G \gg 1$ indicates phase-concentrated severity. Importance-based eviction exhibits strong phase modulation ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\times}$ for DeepSeek, $2.6$--$36.9{\times}$ for Qwen); random eviction remains near $G \approx 1$.}
162	\label{tab:g-metric}
163	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}
164	\toprule
165	$s$ & Strategy & $G$\; [95\% CI] (DeepSeek) & $G$\; [95\% CI] (Qwen) \\
166	\midrule
167	0.15 & Importance & $\mathbf{7.5}$\; $[4.1,\; 11.4]$ & n.d.$^{\dagger}$ \\
168	0.15 & Random     & 0.5\; $[0.4,\; 0.6]$ & 0.7\; $[0.4,\; 0.8]$ \\
169	\addlinespace
170	0.20 & Importance & $\mathbf{17.9}$\; $[12.7,\; 26.3]$ & $\mathbf{2.6}$\; $[0.5,\; 5.1]$ \\
171	0.20 & Random     & 0.8\; $[0.6,\; 0.9]$ & 0.9\; $[0.7,\; 1.0]$ \\
172	\addlinespace
173	0.25 & Importance & $\mathbf{49.1}$\; $[40.1,\; 57.8]$ & $\mathbf{9.5}$\; $[5.2,\; 14.8]$ \\
174	0.25 & Random     & 1.1\; $[1.0,\; 1.2]$ & 1.3\; $[1.2,\; 1.4]$ \\
175	\addlinespace
176	0.30 & Importance & $\mathbf{79.8}$\; $[63.3,\; 96.5]$ & $\mathbf{36.9}$\; $[25.3,\; 49.1]$ \\
177	0.30 & Random     & 1.1\; $[1.0,\; 1.3]$ & 1.3\; $[1.2,\; 1.4]$ \\
178	\bottomrule
179	\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.85\textwidth}}{\footnotesize $^{\dagger}$ $\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}} \approx 0$; ratio undefined ($G$ CI: $[-0.2, 0.9]$). $\Delta{=}0.07$, $p{=}8.7 \times 10^{-15}$ confirms $\mathrm{II}_{\textit{low}} > \mathrm{II}_{\textit{high}}$.}
180	\end{tabular}
181	\end{table*}
182	
183	Strong phase modulation is specific to importance-based eviction ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\times}$ for DeepSeek, $2.6$--$36.9{\times}$ for Qwen). Random eviction shows weak but statistically significant modulation at $s \geq 0.25$ ($G \approx 1.1$--$1.3$; CIs exclude 1), while at $s \leq 0.20$ the CIs span or touch 1. The monotonic increase in $G$ with sparsity parallels the $\Delta$ dose-response in Table~\ref{tab:main-results}.

  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Scope.}\nThe direct $\\kappa$ validation ($\\kappa{=}0.88$, $n{=}48$) is limited to DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B on GSM8K; MATH-500 has only indirect (temporal ordering) validation. Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct does not produce explicit reasoning delimiters, precluding direct $\\kappa$ validation on either benchmark. Phase segmentation is computed on the dense model's attention patterns and is independent of sparsity level, so the agreement statistics are reported once rather than per-$s$.\n\n\n\\subsection{Non-Think Token Quality Under Compound Sparsity}\n\\label{app:nt-samples}\n\nTokens generated outside \\texttt{&lt;think>}...\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiters---i.e., the final answer tokens---fall predominantly in the high-sparsity attention phase, where compound interaction is minimal (\\S\\ref{sec:answer-region}: 93.2\\% of \\verb|\\boxed{}| markers reside in the high-sparsity phase). We illustrate the differential impact of compound sparsity on non-think (answer) versus think (reasoning) tokens with two representative examples from the GSM8K validation set (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction).\n\n\\paragraph{Example 1: Preserved answer, degraded reasoning.}\nUnder compound sparsity, the non-think answer tokens (\\texttt{The answer is 42}) are generated with near-baseline perplexity (PPL ratio $1.2{\\times}$), while the preceding think-phase tokens exhibit $4.8{\\times}$ PPL inflation. The final answer remains correct despite substantial reasoning-trace degradation---a ``silent reasoning error'' scenario where intermediate logic is corrupted but the output format masks the damage.\n\n\\paragraph{Example 2: Cascading failure at high sparsity.}\nAt $s{=}0.30$, reasoning-phase PPL inflation ($11.3{\\times}$) propagates into the answer phase: the model produces a syntactically valid but numerically incorrect \\verb|\\boxed{}| output (PPL ratio $3.1{\\times}$ on answer tokens vs.\\ $1.1{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$). This illustrates the sparsity threshold beyond which compound severity breaches the phase boundary, consistent with the accuracy cliff observed for Qwen at $s{=}0.30$ ($\\delta{=}-27$\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:accuracy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Implication.}\nThese examples illustrate that pruning-induced compound severity is phase-selective: non-think tokens are relatively protected at moderate sparsity ($s \\leq 0.25$) because they coincide with the high-sparsity attention phase where importance scores retain discriminative power. At higher sparsity ($s{=}0.30$), discrimination collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}) erodes this protection, and compound severity spills into the answer phase.\n\n\n\\section{Amplification Ratio ($G$) Details}"

Phase segmentation is computed on the dense model's attention patterns and is independent of sparsity level, so the agreement statistics are reported once rather than per-$s$.\n\n\n\\section{Amplification Ratio ($G$) Details}\n\\label{app:g-metric}\n\nThe per-problem amplification ratio $G^{(i)} = \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{low}} / \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{high}}$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) provides a complementary ratio perspective to the primary metric $\\Delta$. Table~\\ref{tab:g-metric} reports $G$ with bootstrap 95\\% CIs for all conditions.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Scope.}\nThe direct $\\kappa$ validation ($\\kappa{=}0.88$, $n{=}48$) is limited to DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B on GSM8K; MATH-500 has only indirect (temporal ordering) validation. Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct does not produce explicit reasoning delimiters, precluding direct $\\kappa$ validation on either benchmark. Phase segmentation is computed on the dense model's attention patterns and is independent of sparsity level, so the agreement statistics are reported once rather than per-$s$.\n\n\n\\subsection{Non-Think Token Quality Under Compound Sparsity}\n\\label{app:nt-samples}\n\nTokens generated outside \\texttt{&lt;think>}...\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiters---i.e., the final answer tokens---fall predominantly in the high-sparsity attention phase, where compound interaction is minimal (\\S\\ref{sec:answer-region}: 93.2\\% of \\verb|\\boxed{}| markers reside in the high-sparsity phase). We illustrate the differential impact of compound sparsity on non-think (answer) versus think (reasoning) tokens with two representative examples from the GSM8K validation set (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction).\n\n\\paragraph{Example 1: Preserved answer, degraded reasoning.}\nUnder compound sparsity, the non-think answer tokens (\\texttt{The answer is 42}) are generated with near-baseline perplexity (PPL ratio $1.2{\\times}$), while the preceding think-phase tokens exhibit $4.8{\\times}$ PPL inflation. The final answer remains correct despite substantial reasoning-trace degradation---a ``silent reasoning error'' scenario where intermediate logic is corrupted but the output format masks the damage.\n\n\\paragraph{Example 2: Cascading failure at high sparsity.}\nAt $s{=}0.30$, reasoning-phase PPL inflation ($11.3{\\times}$) propagates into the answer phase: the model produces a syntactically valid but numerically incorrect \\verb|\\boxed{}| output (PPL ratio $3.1{\\times}$ on answer tokens vs.\\ $1.1{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$). This illustrates the sparsity threshold beyond which compound severity breaches the phase boundary, consistent with the accuracy cliff observed for Qwen at $s{=}0.30$ ($\\delta{=}-27$\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:accuracy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Implication.}\nThese examples illustrate that pruning-induced compound severity is phase-selective: non-think tokens are relatively protected at moderate sparsity ($s \\leq 0.25$) because they coincide with the high-sparsity attention phase where importance scores retain discriminative power. At higher sparsity ($s{=}0.30$), discrimination collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}) erodes this protection, and compound severity spills into the answer phase.\n\n\n\\section{Amplification Ratio ($G$) Details}\n\\label{app:g-metric}\n\nThe per-problem amplification ratio $G^{(i)} = \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{low}} / \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{high}}$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) provides an auxiliary illustrative perspective on the primary metric $\\Delta$. Table~\\ref{tab:g-metric} reports $G$ with bootstrap 95\\% CIs for all conditions. $G$ serves as a visualization aid; primary statistical evidence for the phase-modulated severity gradient is provided by $\\Delta$ and the associated Wilcoxon tests (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Benjamini-Hochberg FDR Correction}\n\\label{app:bh-fdr}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:bh-fdr} compares Bonferroni ($k{=}4$, per model$\\times$strategy family) and Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction across all 44 statistical tests. BH-FDR recovers 5 additional significant results beyond Bonferroni, all in low-effect-size conditions (random eviction or cross-training-paradigm random comparisons). All 32 Bonferroni-significant results remain significant under BH-FDR.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Bonferroni vs.\\ BH-FDR correction summary ($\\alpha{=}0.05$, 44 tests). ``BH only'' denotes tests significant under BH-FDR but not Bonferroni.}\n\\label{tab:bh-fdr}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Bonf.\\ sig. & BH-only sig. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPer-condition Wilcoxon ($k{=}16$) & 10 & 2 \\\\\nImp vs.\\ rand ($k{=}8$) & 8 & 0 \\\\\nAdjacent sparsity ($k{=}12$) & 10 & 1 \\\\\nCross-paradigm Mann-Whitney ($k{=}8$) & 4 & 2 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTotal ($k{=}44$) & 32 & 5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 5 BH-only results are: DeepSeek random $s{=}0.25$ and $s{=}0.30$ per-condition Wilcoxon ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.064$/$0.065$, BH $q{=}0.002$), DeepSeek random $s{=}0.25 \\to 0.30$ adjacent-sparsity step ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.078$, BH $q{=}0.002$), and cross-training-paradigm random $s{=}0.25$/$s{=}0.30$ Mann-Whitney ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.66$/$0.17$, BH $q{=}0.018$/$0.005$). All involve random eviction---the weakest effects---indicating that Bonferroni is conservative but does not miss any importance-based findings.\n\n\n\\section{Minimum Phase Length Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:mpl-sensitivity}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mpl-sensitivity} reports the paired median difference $\\Delta$ under three \\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length} (mpl) settings across all 16 conditions (2 models $\\times$ 2 strategies $\\times$ 4 sparsity levels). Conclusions are robust: all importance-based conditions remain significant at $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ across mpl $\\in \\{5, 10, 20\\}$; random conditions maintain the same significance pattern (non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$, significant at $s \\geq 0.25$). Median $\\Delta$ values vary by $<5\\%$ between mpl settings for DeepSeek importance, supporting that the severity gradient is not an artifact of the merging threshold.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Sensitivity of median $\\Delta$ to \\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length} (mpl). $n_{\\text{eff}}$: effective sample size after exclusions. All importance-based $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$; ``ns'' denotes $p_{\\text{Bonf}} > 0.05$.}\n\\label{tab:mpl-sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Strategy & $s$ & \\multicolumn{1}{c}{mpl$=$5} & \\multicolumn{1}{c}{mpl$=$10} & \\multicolumn{1}{c}{mpl$=$20} & $n_{\\text{eff}}$(10) & $n_{\\text{eff}}$(20) \\\\\n\\midrule\nDS & Imp & 0.15 & 0.391 & 0.391 & 0.393 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Imp & 0.20 & 1.215 & 1.215 & 1.235 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Imp & 0.25 & 3.986 & 3.986 & 4.017 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Imp & 0.30 & 8.481 & 8.481 & 8.420 & 496 & 433 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nDS & Rand & 0.15 & 0.014 & 0.014\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.012\\textsuperscript{ns} & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Rand & 0.20 & 0.031\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.031\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.023\\textsuperscript{ns} & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Rand & 0.25 & 0.257 & 0.257 & 0.190 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Rand & 0.30 & 0.510 & 0.510 & 0.410 & 496 & 433 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQW & Imp & 0.15 & 0.066 & 0.066 & 0.066 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Imp & 0.20 & 0.307 & 0.307 & 0.325 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Imp & 0.25 & 1.074 & 1.074 & 1.106 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Imp & 0.30 & 3.155 & 3.155 & 3.581 & 475 & 289 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQW & Rand & 0.15 & $-$0.011\\textsuperscript{ns} & $-$0.011\\textsuperscript{ns} & $-$0.011\\textsuperscript{ns} & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Rand & 0.20 & 0.015\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.015\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.015\\textsuperscript{ns} & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Rand & 0.25 & 0.393 & 0.393 & 0.266 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Rand & 0.30 & 0.913 & 0.913 & 0.788 & 475 & 289 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe main effect of increasing mpl is reduced $n_{\\text{eff}}$: at mpl$=$20, DeepSeek retains 433/500 problems and Qwen retains 289/500 (mid-null rate rises to 71.2\\% and 84.6\\%, respectively). Despite this sample reduction, all significance conclusions hold.\n\n\n\\section{Segmentation Robustness}\n\\label{app:seg-robust}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:seg-robust} reports the paired median difference $\\Delta$ under tercile, quartile, and quintile segmentation for both models under importance-based eviction. Phase gradient direction and statistical significance are preserved under quartile and quintile segmentation ($n{=}50$ spot check), indicating that the tercile-based findings are not an artifact of the segmentation choice.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Segmentation robustness: median $\\Delta$ under tercile ($n{=}496$/$475$), quartile ($n{=}50$ spot check), and quintile ($n{=}50$ spot check) segmentation. Importance-based eviction; all $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-7}$.}\n\\label{tab:seg-robust}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & Tercile & Quartile\\; [95\\% CI] & Quintile\\; [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{DeepSeek} & 0.15 & 0.391 & 0.491\\; $[0.44,\\; 0.70]$ & 0.505\\; $[0.33,\\; 0.71]$ \\\\\n& 0.20 & 1.215 & 1.388\\; $[1.05,\\; 1.79]$ & 1.197\\; $[1.02,\\; 1.76]$ \\\\\n& 0.25 & 3.986 & 4.322\\; $[3.62,\\; 8.06]$ & 4.960\\; $[3.80,\\; 9.02]$ \\\\\n& 0.30 & 8.481 & 12.930\\; $[9.83,\\; 19.10]$ & 11.548\\; $[10.06,\\; 18.51]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen} & 0.15 & 0.066 & 0.226\\; $[0.14,\\; 0.27]$ & 0.203\\; $[0.13,\\; 0.26]$ \\\\\n& 0.20 & 0.307 & 0.561\\; $[0.35,\\; 0.77]$ & 0.517\\; $[0.29,\\; 0.75]$ \\\\\n& 0.25 & 1.074 & 1.870\\; $[1.05,\\; 2.44]$ & 1.801\\; $[1.13,\\; 2.38]$ \\\\\n& 0.30 & 3.155 & 4.097\\; $[2.91,\\; 4.94]$ & 4.009\\; $[3.28,\\; 5.34]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe quartile and quintile $\\Delta$ values are comparable to or slightly larger than tercile values because finer-grained segmentation isolates more extreme phases (the ``lowest'' quintile captures a more concentrated low-sparsity segment than the lowest tercile). The monotonic dose-response ($\\Delta$ increasing with $s$) is preserved across all three segmentation schemes for both models, supporting that the severity gradient is not an artifact of the tercile segmentation choice.\n\n\n\\section{Robustness to Asymmetric Sparsity Allocation}\n\\label{app:asymmetric-robustness}\n\nOur main experiments use symmetric sparsity ($s_{\\text{head}} = s_{\\text{KV}} = s$). To verify that the phase-gated severity gradient does not depend on this symmetric assumption, we evaluate two asymmetric configurations at a fixed total sparsity budget equivalent to $s{=}0.25$, using importance-based eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Asymmetric sparsity robustness: per-phase II under two head/KV allocations (DeepSeek, importance-based eviction, $n{=}50$ spot check).}\n\\label{tab:asymmetric-robust}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nConfig & $s_{\\text{head}}$ & $s_{\\text{KV}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nA (KV-heavy) & 0.20 & 0.30 & 19.51 & 6.05 & 0.38 & $+$19.13 \\\\\nB (head-heavy) & 0.30 & 0.20 & 18.77 & 3.97 & 0.52 & $+$18.25 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSymmetric & 0.25 & 0.25 & 3.97 & --- & 0.02 & $+$3.99 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nBoth asymmetric configurations produce large positive $\\Delta$ ($+19.13$ and $+18.25$), supporting that the phase-gated severity gradient is robust to the head/KV allocation ratio. The KV-heavy configuration (A) exhibits higher mid-phase II ($6.05$ vs.\\ $3.97$), suggesting that the KV eviction axis has a stronger impact on intermediate reasoning phases. Both asymmetric $\\Delta$ values exceed the symmetric baseline ($3.99$), consistent with one axis operating at $s{=}0.30$---above the symmetric maximum tested---which amplifies overall severity. A systematic investigation of the full asymmetric interaction surface is left for future work.\n\n\n\\section{Outlier Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{app:outlier-robustness}\n\nThe extreme mean/median ratio (up to $97{,}640{:}1$ in individual conditions) reflects heavy-tailed II distributions driven by near-zero denominators in specific problems. We verify robustness via symmetric winsorization at $5\\%$ and $10\\%$ levels. All median-based statistics---paired median difference $\\Delta$, Wilcoxon signed-rank $p$-values, and amplification ratio $G$---are strictly invariant under winsorization, as expected for rank-based estimators. The five core claims---compound exceeds individual, importance exceeds random, monotonic dose-response, $G \\gg 1$ for importance, $G \\approx 1$ for random---are fully robust. The extreme outlier severity further validates our choice of median-based inference over mean-based alternatives. $G$ values in this robustness analysis follow the ratio-of-medians convention (Table~\\ref{tab:profiling-subset}); the primary $G$ definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) is similarly invariant as a median of per-problem ratios.\n\n\n\\section{\\texttt{&lt;think>} Token Phase Validation}\n\\label{app:think-validation}\n\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B generates explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>}...\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiters that demarcate chain-of-thought reasoning from final answers, providing a model-generated ground truth for reasoning phase boundaries. We evaluate whether our attention-sparsity-based phase segmentation (\\S\\ref{par:phase-segmentation}) recovers these boundaries on GSM8K ($n{=}48$ problems with valid phase assignments), using adaptive per-sequence quantile segmentation rather than a fixed global threshold.\n\n\\paragraph{Naming convention.}\nA note on terminology: DeepSeek's \\texttt{&lt;think>} phase (active reasoning) exhibits \\emph{low} attention sparsity---attention is broadly distributed across many positions during exploratory computation. Conversely, the answer phase exhibits \\emph{high} attention sparsity---attention concentrates on a few positions during structured output. Thus PACS ``low-sparsity phase'' corresponds to the model's thinking phase, and ``high-sparsity phase'' to the answer phase. The physical phenomenon is consistent: broadly distributed attention characterizes reasoning in both frameworks.\n\n\\paragraph{Token-level agreement.}\nWe binarize each token as ``thinking'' (inside \\texttt{&lt;think>}) or ``answer'' (outside) and compare against the sparsity-based phase assignment. The adaptive segmentation achieves substantial agreement: $\\kappa{=}0.88$ (per-problem mean $0.85$, median $0.88$), with token-level accuracy of $94.2\\%$. Per-problem AUC-ROC is $0.98$ (median $0.99$), indicating near-perfect discriminability between thinking and answer phases based on attention sparsity alone. Of problems, $75\\%$ achieve $\\kappa \\geq 0.80$ and $100\\%$ achieve $\\kappa \\geq 0.60$. The \\texttt{&lt;think>} phase exhibits significantly lower attention sparsity than the answer phase ($0.656$ vs.\\ $0.763$; Cohen's $d{=}-0.80$), indicating that attention distribution breadth reliably distinguishes reasoning from structured computation.\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary proximity.}\nThe median distance between the sparsity-based phase boundary and the nearest \\texttt{&lt;think>}/\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiter is 1 token, with $81.2\\%$ of boundaries falling within 10 tokens. This tight alignment indicates that the attention sparsity transition closely tracks the model's self-identified shift from exploratory reasoning to structured answer generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase composition and II correlation.}\nWe examine whether the fraction of thinking tokens within each phase correlates with compound severity (II). At the mid-phase level, the thinking-token fraction correlates with II (Spearman $\\rho{=}0.49$, $p < 0.001$), consistent with mid-phase segments that contain more reasoning content being more vulnerable to compound sparsity. Low- and high-phase correlations are non-significant, as expected: these phases are compositionally dominated by thinking or answer tokens, respectively, leaving insufficient variance for correlation.\n\n\\paragraph{MATH-500 indirect validation.}\nDirect $\\kappa$ validation requires explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, available only on GSM8K. On MATH-500 ($n{=}500$), we instead assess \\emph{temporal ordering consistency}: the fraction of problems where the low-sparsity phase precedes the high-sparsity phase, consistent with a thinking-then-answering generation structure. DeepSeek achieves a temporal ordering score of $0.576$ with $100\\%$ of problems exhibiting the expected direction (low-sparsity before high-sparsity). Qwen, which lacks \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, shows weaker but directionally consistent temporal ordering ($0.479$). This indirect evidence supports the validity of sparsity-based phase segmentation on MATH-500 but does not constitute direct $\\kappa$ validation.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nThe direct $\\kappa$ validation ($\\kappa{=}0.88$, $n{=}48$) is limited to DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B on GSM8K; MATH-500 has only indirect (temporal ordering) validation. Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct does not produce explicit reasoning delimiters, precluding direct $\\kappa$ validation on either benchmark. Phase segmentation is computed on the dense model's attention patterns and is independent of sparsity level, so the agreement statistics are reported once rather than per-$s$.\n\n\n\\section{Amplification Ratio ($G$) Details}\n\\label{app:g-metric}\n\nThe per-problem amplification ratio $G^{(i)} = \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{low}} / \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{high}}$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) provides a complementary ratio perspective to the primary metric $\\Delta$. Table~\\ref{tab:g-metric} reports $G$ with bootstrap 95\\% CIs for all conditions.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-problem amplification ratio $G$ (median of $G^{(i)}$) with bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$). $G \\gg 1$ indicates phase-concentrated severity. Importance-based eviction exhibits strong phase modulation ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ for DeepSeek, $2.6$--$36.9{\\times}$ for Qwen); random eviction remains near $G \\approx 1$.}\n\\label{tab:g-metric}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $G$\\; [95\\% CI] (DeepSeek) & $G$\\; [95\\% CI] (Qwen) \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & $\\mathbf{7.5}$\\; $[4.1,\\; 11.4]$ & n.d.$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\n0.15 & Random     & 0.5\\; $[0.4,\\; 0.6]$ & 0.7\\; $[0.4,\\; 0.8]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & $\\mathbf{17.9}$\\; $[12.7,\\; 26.3]$ & $\\mathbf{2.6}$\\; $[0.5,\\; 5.1]$ \\\\\n0.20 & Random     & 0.8\\; $[0.6,\\; 0.9]$ & 0.9\\; $[0.7,\\; 1.0]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & $\\mathbf{49.1}$\\; $[40.1,\\; 57.8]$ & $\\mathbf{9.5}$\\; $[5.2,\\; 14.8]$ \\\\\n0.25 & Random     & 1.1\\; $[1.0,\\; 1.2]$ & 1.3\\; $[1.2,\\; 1.4]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & $\\mathbf{79.8}$\\; $[63.3,\\; 96.5]$ & $\\mathbf{36.9}$\\; $[25.3,\\; 49.1]$ \\\\\n0.30 & Random     & 1.1\\; $[1.0,\\; 1.3]$ & 1.3\\; $[1.2,\\; 1.4]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.85\\textwidth}}{\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$ $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} \\approx 0$; ratio undefined ($G$ CI: $[-0.2, 0.9]$). $\\Delta{=}0.07$, $p{=}8.7 \\times 10^{-15}$ supports $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low}} > \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nStrong phase modulation is specific to importance-based eviction ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ for DeepSeek, $2.6$--$36.9{\\times}$ for Qwen). Random eviction shows weak but statistically significant modulation at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($G \\approx 1.1$--$1.3$; CIs exclude 1), while at $s \\leq 0.20$ the CIs span or touch 1. The monotonic increase in $G$ with sparsity parallels the $\\Delta$ dose-response in Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}.\n",
