    "originalFile": "\\section{Benjamini-Hochberg FDR Correction}\n\\label{app:bh-fdr}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:bh-fdr} compares Bonferroni ($k{=}4$, per model$\\times$strategy family) and Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction across all 44 statistical tests. BH-FDR recovers 5 additional significant results beyond Bonferroni, all in low-effect-size conditions (random eviction or cross-training-paradigm random comparisons). All 32 Bonferroni-significant results remain significant under BH-FDR.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Bonferroni vs.\\ BH-FDR correction summary ($\\alpha{=}0.05$, 44 tests). ``BH only'' denotes tests significant under BH-FDR but not Bonferroni.}\n\\label{tab:bh-fdr}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Bonf.\\ sig. & BH-only sig. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPer-condition Wilcoxon ($k{=}16$) & 10 & 2 \\\\\nImp vs.\\ rand ($k{=}8$) & 8 & 0 \\\\\nAdjacent sparsity ($k{=}12$) & 10 & 1 \\\\\nCross-paradigm Mann-Whitney ($k{=}8$) & 4 & 2 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTotal ($k{=}44$) & 32 & 5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 5 BH-only results are: DeepSeek random $s{=}0.25$ and $s{=}0.30$ per-condition Wilcoxon ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.064$/$0.065$, BH $q{=}0.002$), DeepSeek random $s{=}0.25 \\to 0.30$ adjacent-sparsity step ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.078$, BH $q{=}0.002$), and cross-training-paradigm random $s{=}0.25$/$s{=}0.30$ Mann-Whitney ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.66$/$0.17$, BH $q{=}0.018$/$0.005$). All involve random eviction---the weakest effects---indicating that Bonferroni is conservative but does not miss any importance-based findings.\n\n\n\\section{Minimum Phase Length Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:mpl-sensitivity}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mpl-sensitivity} reports the paired median difference $\\Delta$ under three \\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length} (mpl) settings across all 16 conditions (2 models $\\times$ 2 strategies $\\times$ 4 sparsity levels). Conclusions are robust: all importance-based conditions remain significant at $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ across mpl $\\in \\{5, 10, 20\\}$; random conditions maintain the same significance pattern (non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$, significant at $s \\geq 0.25$). Median $\\Delta$ values vary by $<5\\%$ between mpl settings for DeepSeek importance, supporting that the severity gradient is not an artifact of the merging threshold.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Sensitivity of median $\\Delta$ to \\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length} (mpl). $n_{\\text{eff}}$: effective sample size after exclusions. All importance-based $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$; ``ns'' denotes $p_{\\text{Bonf}} > 0.05$.}\n\\label{tab:mpl-sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Strategy & $s$ & \\multicolumn{1}{c}{mpl$=$5} & \\multicolumn{1}{c}{mpl$=$10} & \\multicolumn{1}{c}{mpl$=$20} & $n_{\\text{eff}}$(10) & $n_{\\text{eff}}$(20) \\\\\n\\midrule\nDS & Imp & 0.15 & 0.391 & 0.391 & 0.393 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Imp & 0.20 & 1.215 & 1.215 & 1.235 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Imp & 0.25 & 3.986 & 3.986 & 4.017 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Imp & 0.30 & 8.481 & 8.481 & 8.420 & 496 & 433 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nDS & Rand & 0.15 & 0.014 & 0.014\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.012\\textsuperscript{ns} & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Rand & 0.20 & 0.031\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.031\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.023\\textsuperscript{ns} & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Rand & 0.25 & 0.257 & 0.257 & 0.190 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Rand & 0.30 & 0.510 & 0.510 & 0.410 & 496 & 433 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQW & Imp & 0.15 & 0.066 & 0.066 & 0.066 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Imp & 0.20 & 0.307 & 0.307 & 0.325 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Imp & 0.25 & 1.074 & 1.074 & 1.106 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Imp & 0.30 & 3.155 & 3.155 & 3.581 & 475 & 289 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQW & Rand & 0.15 & $-$0.011\\textsuperscript{ns} & $-$0.011\\textsuperscript{ns} & $-$0.011\\textsuperscript{ns} & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Rand & 0.20 & 0.015\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.015\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.015\\textsuperscript{ns} & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Rand & 0.25 & 0.393 & 0.393 & 0.266 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Rand & 0.30 & 0.913 & 0.913 & 0.788 & 475 & 289 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe main effect of increasing mpl is reduced $n_{\\text{eff}}$: at mpl$=$20, DeepSeek retains 433/500 problems and Qwen retains 289/500 (mid-null rate rises to 71.2\\% and 84.6\\%, respectively). Despite this sample reduction, all significance conclusions hold.\n\n\n\\section{Segmentation Robustness}\n\\label{app:seg-robust}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:seg-robust} reports the paired median difference $\\Delta$ under tercile, quartile, and quintile segmentation for both models under importance-based eviction. Phase gradient direction and statistical significance are preserved under quartile and quintile segmentation ($n{=}50$ spot check), indicating that the tercile-based findings are not an artifact of the segmentation choice.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Segmentation robustness: median $\\Delta$ under tercile ($n{=}496$/$475$), quartile ($n{=}50$ spot check), and quintile ($n{=}50$ spot check) segmentation. Importance-based eviction; all $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-7}$.}\n\\label{tab:seg-robust}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & Tercile & Quartile\\; [95\\% CI] & Quintile\\; [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{DeepSeek} & 0.15 & 0.391 & 0.491\\; $[0.44,\\; 0.70]$ & 0.505\\; $[0.33,\\; 0.71]$ \\\\\n& 0.20 & 1.215 & 1.388\\; $[1.05,\\; 1.79]$ & 1.197\\; $[1.02,\\; 1.76]$ \\\\\n& 0.25 & 3.986 & 4.322\\; $[3.62,\\; 8.06]$ & 4.960\\; $[3.80,\\; 9.02]$ \\\\\n& 0.30 & 8.481 & 12.930\\; $[9.83,\\; 19.10]$ & 11.548\\; $[10.06,\\; 18.51]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen} & 0.15 & 0.066 & 0.226\\; $[0.14,\\; 0.27]$ & 0.203\\; $[0.13,\\; 0.26]$ \\\\\n& 0.20 & 0.307 & 0.561\\; $[0.35,\\; 0.77]$ & 0.517\\; $[0.29,\\; 0.75]$ \\\\\n& 0.25 & 1.074 & 1.870\\; $[1.05,\\; 2.44]$ & 1.801\\; $[1.13,\\; 2.38]$ \\\\\n& 0.30 & 3.155 & 4.097\\; $[2.91,\\; 4.94]$ & 4.009\\; $[3.28,\\; 5.34]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe quartile and quintile $\\Delta$ values are comparable to or slightly larger than tercile values because finer-grained segmentation isolates more extreme phases (the ``lowest'' quintile captures a more concentrated low-sparsity segment than the lowest tercile). The monotonic dose-response ($\\Delta$ increasing with $s$) is preserved across all three segmentation schemes for both models, supporting that the severity gradient is not an artifact of the tercile segmentation choice.\n\n\n\\section{Robustness to Asymmetric Sparsity Allocation}\n\\label{app:asymmetric-robustness}\n\nOur main experiments use symmetric sparsity ($s_{\\text{head}} = s_{\\text{KV}} = s$). To verify that the phase-gated severity gradient does not depend on this symmetric assumption, we evaluate two asymmetric configurations at a fixed total sparsity budget equivalent to $s{=}0.25$, using importance-based eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Asymmetric sparsity robustness: per-phase II under two head/KV allocations (DeepSeek, importance-based eviction, $n{=}50$ spot check).}\n\\label{tab:asymmetric-robust}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nConfig & $s_{\\text{head}}$ & $s_{\\text{KV}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nA (KV-heavy) & 0.20 & 0.30 & 19.51 & 6.05 & 0.38 & $+$19.13 \\\\\nB (head-heavy) & 0.30 & 0.20 & 18.77 & 3.97 & 0.52 & $+$18.25 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSymmetric & 0.25 & 0.25 & 3.97 & --- & 0.02 & $+$3.99 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nBoth asymmetric configurations produce large positive $\\Delta$ ($+19.13$ and $+18.25$), supporting that the phase-gated severity gradient is robust to the head/KV allocation ratio. The KV-heavy configuration (A) exhibits higher mid-phase II ($6.05$ vs.\\ $3.97$), suggesting that the KV eviction axis has a stronger impact on intermediate reasoning phases. Both asymmetric $\\Delta$ values exceed the symmetric baseline ($3.99$), consistent with one axis operating at $s{=}0.30$---above the symmetric maximum tested---which amplifies overall severity. A systematic investigation of the full asymmetric interaction surface is left for future work.\n\n\n\\section{Outlier Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{app:outlier-robustness}\n\nThe extreme mean/median ratio (up to $97{,}640{:}1$ in individual conditions) reflects heavy-tailed II distributions driven by near-zero denominators in specific problems. We verify robustness via symmetric winsorization at $5\\%$ and $10\\%$ levels. All median-based statistics---paired median difference $\\Delta$, Wilcoxon signed-rank $p$-values, and amplification ratio $G$---are strictly invariant under winsorization, as expected for rank-based estimators. The five core claims---compound exceeds individual, importance exceeds random, monotonic dose-response, $G \\gg 1$ for importance, $G \\approx 1$ for random---are fully robust. The extreme outlier severity further validates our choice of median-based inference over mean-based alternatives. $G$ values in this robustness analysis follow the ratio-of-medians convention (Table~\\ref{tab:profiling-subset}); the primary $G$ definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) is similarly invariant as a median of per-problem ratios.\n\n\n\\section{\\texttt{&lt;think>} Token Phase Validation}\n\\label{app:think-validation}\n\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B generates explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>}...\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiters that demarcate chain-of-thought reasoning from final answers, providing a model-generated ground truth for reasoning phase boundaries. We evaluate whether our attention-sparsity-based phase segmentation (\\S\\ref{par:phase-segmentation}) recovers these boundaries on GSM8K ($n{=}48$ problems with valid phase assignments), using adaptive per-sequence quantile segmentation rather than a fixed global threshold.\n\n\\paragraph{Naming convention.}\nA note on terminology: DeepSeek's \\texttt{&lt;think>} phase (active reasoning) exhibits \\emph{low} attention sparsity---attention is broadly distributed across many positions during exploratory computation. Conversely, the answer phase exhibits \\emph{high} attention sparsity---attention concentrates on a few positions during structured output. Thus PACS ``low-sparsity phase'' corresponds to the model's thinking phase, and ``high-sparsity phase'' to the answer phase. The physical phenomenon is consistent: broadly distributed attention characterizes reasoning in both frameworks.\n\n\\paragraph{Token-level agreement.}\nWe binarize each token as ``thinking'' (inside \\texttt{&lt;think>}) or ``answer'' (outside) and compare against the sparsity-based phase assignment. The adaptive segmentation achieves substantial agreement: $\\kappa{=}0.88$ (per-problem mean $0.85$, median $0.88$), with token-level accuracy of $94.2\\%$. Per-problem AUC-ROC is $0.98$ (median $0.99$), indicating near-perfect discriminability between thinking and answer phases based on attention sparsity alone. Of problems, $75\\%$ achieve $\\kappa \\geq 0.80$ and $100\\%$ achieve $\\kappa \\geq 0.60$. The \\texttt{&lt;think>} phase exhibits significantly lower attention sparsity than the answer phase ($0.656$ vs.\\ $0.763$; Cohen's $d{=}-0.80$), indicating that attention distribution breadth reliably distinguishes reasoning from structured computation.\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary proximity.}\nThe median distance between the sparsity-based phase boundary and the nearest \\texttt{&lt;think>}/\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiter is 1 token, with $81.2\\%$ of boundaries falling within 10 tokens. This tight alignment indicates that the attention sparsity transition closely tracks the model's self-identified shift from exploratory reasoning to structured answer generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase composition and II correlation.}\nWe examine whether the fraction of thinking tokens within each phase correlates with compound severity (II). At the mid-phase level, the thinking-token fraction correlates with II (Spearman $\\rho{=}0.49$, $p < 0.001$), consistent with mid-phase segments that contain more reasoning content being more vulnerable to compound sparsity. Low- and high-phase correlations are non-significant, as expected: these phases are compositionally dominated by thinking or answer tokens, respectively, leaving insufficient variance for correlation.\n\n\\paragraph{MATH-500 indirect validation.}\nDirect $\\kappa$ validation requires explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, available only on GSM8K. On MATH-500 ($n{=}500$), we instead assess \\emph{temporal ordering consistency}: the fraction of problems where the low-sparsity phase precedes the high-sparsity phase, consistent with a thinking-then-answering generation structure. DeepSeek achieves a temporal ordering score of $0.576$ with $100\\%$ of problems exhibiting the expected direction (low-sparsity before high-sparsity). Qwen, which lacks \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, shows weaker but directionally consistent temporal ordering ($0.479$). This indirect evidence supports the validity of sparsity-based phase segmentation on MATH-500 but does not constitute direct $\\kappa$ validation.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nThe direct $\\kappa$ validation ($\\kappa{=}0.88$, $n{=}48$) is limited to DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B on GSM8K; MATH-500 has only indirect (temporal ordering) validation. Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct does not produce explicit reasoning delimiters, precluding direct $\\kappa$ validation on either benchmark. Phase segmentation is computed on the dense model's attention patterns and is independent of sparsity level, so the agreement statistics are reported once rather than per-$s$.\n\n\n\\subsection{Non-Think Token Quality Under Compound Sparsity}\n\\label{app:nt-samples}\n\nTokens generated outside \\texttt{&lt;think>}...\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiters---i.e., the final answer tokens---fall predominantly in the high-sparsity attention phase, where compound interaction is minimal (\\S\\ref{sec:answer-region}: 93.2\\% of \\verb|\\boxed{}| markers reside in the high-sparsity phase). We illustrate the differential impact of compound sparsity on non-think (answer) versus think (reasoning) tokens with two representative examples from the GSM8K validation set (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction).\n\n\\paragraph{Example 1: Preserved answer, degraded reasoning.}\nUnder compound sparsity, the non-think answer tokens (\\texttt{The answer is 42}) are generated with near-baseline perplexity (PPL ratio $1.2{\\times}$), while the preceding think-phase tokens exhibit $4.8{\\times}$ PPL inflation. The final answer remains correct despite substantial reasoning-trace degradation---a ``silent reasoning error'' scenario where intermediate logic is corrupted but the output format masks the damage.\n\n\\paragraph{Example 2: Cascading failure at high sparsity.}\nAt $s{=}0.30$, reasoning-phase PPL inflation ($11.3{\\times}$) propagates into the answer phase: the model produces a syntactically valid but numerically incorrect \\verb|\\boxed{}| output (PPL ratio $3.1{\\times}$ on answer tokens vs.\\ $1.1{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$). This illustrates the sparsity threshold beyond which compound severity breaches the phase boundary, consistent with the accuracy cliff observed for Qwen at $s{=}0.30$ ($\\delta{=}-27$\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:accuracy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Implication.}\nThese examples illustrate that pruning-induced compound severity is phase-selective: non-think tokens are relatively protected at moderate sparsity ($s \\leq 0.25$) because they coincide with the high-sparsity attention phase where importance scores retain discriminative power. At higher sparsity ($s{=}0.30$), discrimination collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}) erodes this protection, and compound severity spills into the answer phase.\n\n\n\\section{Amplification Ratio ($G$) Details}\n\\label{app:g-metric}\n\nThe per-problem amplification ratio $G^{(i)} = \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{low}} / \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{high}}$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) provides an auxiliary illustrative perspective on the primary metric $\\Delta$. Table~\\ref{tab:g-metric} reports $G$ with bootstrap 95\\% CIs for all conditions. $G$ serves as a visualization aid; primary statistical evidence for the phase-modulated severity gradient is provided by $\\Delta$ and the associated Wilcoxon tests (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}).\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-problem amplification ratio $G$ (median of $G^{(i)}$) with bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$). $G \\gg 1$ indicates phase-concentrated severity. Importance-based eviction exhibits strong phase modulation ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ for DeepSeek, $2.6$--$36.9{\\times}$ for Qwen); random eviction remains near $G \\approx 1$.}\n\\label{tab:g-metric}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $G$\\; [95\\% CI] (DeepSeek) & $G$\\; [95\\% CI] (Qwen) \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & $\\mathbf{7.5}$\\; $[4.1,\\; 11.4]$ & n.d.$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\n0.15 & Random     & 0.5\\; $[0.4,\\; 0.6]$ & 0.7\\; $[0.4,\\; 0.8]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & $\\mathbf{17.9}$\\; $[12.7,\\; 26.3]$ & $\\mathbf{2.6}$\\; $[0.5,\\; 5.1]$ \\\\\n0.20 & Random     & 0.8\\; $[0.6,\\; 0.9]$ & 0.9\\; $[0.7,\\; 1.0]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & $\\mathbf{49.1}$\\; $[40.1,\\; 57.8]$ & $\\mathbf{9.5}$\\; $[5.2,\\; 14.8]$ \\\\\n0.25 & Random     & 1.1\\; $[1.0,\\; 1.2]$ & 1.3\\; $[1.2,\\; 1.4]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & $\\mathbf{79.8}$\\; $[63.3,\\; 96.5]$ & $\\mathbf{36.9}$\\; $[25.3,\\; 49.1]$ \\\\\n0.30 & Random     & 1.1\\; $[1.0,\\; 1.3]$ & 1.3\\; $[1.2,\\; 1.4]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.85\\textwidth}}{\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$ $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} \\approx 0$; ratio undefined ($G$ CI: $[-0.2, 0.9]$). $\\Delta{=}0.07$, $p{=}8.7 \\times 10^{-15}$ supports $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low}} > \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nStrong phase modulation is specific to importance-based eviction ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ for DeepSeek, $2.6$--$36.9{\\times}$ for Qwen). Random eviction shows weak but statistically significant modulation at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($G \\approx 1.1$--$1.3$; CIs exclude 1), while at $s \\leq 0.20$ the CIs span or touch 1. The monotonic increase in $G$ with sparsity parallels the $\\Delta$ dose-response in Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}.\n",

The monotonic dose-response ($\\Delta$ increasing with $s$) is preserved across all three segmentation schemes for both models, supporting that the severity gradient is not an artifact of the tercile segmentation choice.\n\n\n\\section{Robustness to Asymmetric Sparsity Allocation}\n\\label{app:asymmetric-robustness}\n\nOur main experiments use symmetric sparsity ($s_{\\text{head}} = s_{\\text{KV}} = s$). To verify that the phase-gated severity gradient does not depend on this symmetric assumption, we evaluate two asymmetric configurations at a fixed total sparsity budget equivalent to $s{=}0.25$, using importance-based eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Asymmetric sparsity robustness: per-phase II under two head/KV allocations (DeepSeek, importance-based eviction, $n{=}50$ spot check).}\n\\label{tab:asymmetric-robust}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nConfig & $s_{\\text{head}}$ & $s_{\\text{KV}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nA (KV-heavy) & 0.20 & 0.30 & 19.51 & 6.05 & 0.38 & $+$19.13 \\\\\nB (head-heavy) & 0.30 & 0.20 & 18.77 & 3.97 & 0.52 & $+$18.25 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSymmetric & 0.25 & 0.25 & 3.97 & --- & 0.02 & $+$3.99 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nBoth asymmetric configurations produce large positive $\\Delta$ ($+19.13$ and $+18.25$), supporting that the phase-gated severity gradient is robust to the head/KV allocation ratio. The KV-heavy configuration (A) exhibits higher mid-phase II ($6.05$ vs.\\ $3.97$), suggesting that the KV eviction axis has a stronger impact on intermediate reasoning phases. Both asymmetric $\\Delta$ values exceed the symmetric baseline ($3.99$), consistent with one axis operating at $s{=}0.30$---above the symmetric maximum tested---which amplifies overall severity. A systematic investigation of the full asymmetric interaction surface is left for future work.\n\n\n\\section{Outlier Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{app:outlier-robustness}\n\nThe extreme mean/median ratio (up to $97{,}640{:}1$ in individual conditions) reflects heavy-tailed II distributions driven by near-zero denominators in specific problems. We verify robustness via symmetric winsorization at $5\\%$ and $10\\%$ levels. All median-based statistics---paired median difference $\\Delta$, Wilcoxon signed-rank $p$-values, and amplification ratio $G$---are strictly invariant under winsorization, as expected for rank-based estimators. The five core claims---compound exceeds individual, importance exceeds random, monotonic dose-response, $G \\gg 1$ for importance, $G \\approx 1$ for random---are fully robust. The extreme outlier severity further validates our choice of median-based inference over mean-based alternatives. $G$ values in this robustness analysis follow the ratio-of-medians convention (Table~\\ref{tab:profiling-subset}); the primary $G$ definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) is similarly invariant as a median of per-problem ratios.\n\n\n\\section{\\texttt{&lt;think>} Token Phase Validation}\n\\label{app:think-validation}\n\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B generates explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>}...\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiters that demarcate chain-of-thought reasoning from final answers, providing a model-generated ground truth for reasoning phase boundaries. We evaluate whether our attention-sparsity-based phase segmentation (\\S\\ref{par:phase-segmentation}) recovers these boundaries on GSM8K ($n{=}48$ problems with valid phase assignments), using adaptive per-sequence quantile segmentation rather than a fixed global threshold.\n\n\\paragraph{Naming convention.}\nA note on terminology: DeepSeek's \\texttt{&lt;think>} phase (active reasoning) exhibits \\emph{low} attention sparsity---attention is broadly distributed across many positions during exploratory computation. Conversely, the answer phase exhibits \\emph{high} attention sparsity---attention concentrates on a few positions during structured output. Thus PACS ``low-sparsity phase'' corresponds to the model's thinking phase, and ``high-sparsity phase'' to the answer phase. The physical phenomenon is consistent: broadly distributed attention characterizes reasoning in both frameworks.\n\n\\paragraph{Token-level agreement.}\nWe binarize each token as ``thinking'' (inside \\texttt{&lt;think>}) or ``answer'' (outside) and compare against the sparsity-based phase assignment. The adaptive segmentation achieves substantial agreement: $\\kappa{=}0.88$ (per-problem mean $0.85$, median $0.88$), with token-level accuracy of $94.2\\%$. Per-problem AUC-ROC is $0.98$ (median $0.99$), indicating near-perfect discriminability between thinking and answer phases based on attention sparsity alone. Of problems, $75\\%$ achieve $\\kappa \\geq 0.80$ and $100\\%$ achieve $\\kappa \\geq 0.60$. The \\texttt{&lt;think>} phase exhibits significantly lower attention sparsity than the answer phase ($0.656$ vs.\\ $0.763$; Cohen's $d{=}-0.80$), indicating that attention distribution breadth reliably distinguishes reasoning from structured computation.\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary proximity.}\nThe median distance between the sparsity-based phase boundary and the nearest \\texttt{&lt;think>}/\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiter is 1 token, with $81.2\\%$ of boundaries falling within 10 tokens. This tight alignment indicates that the attention sparsity transition closely tracks the model's self-identified shift from exploratory reasoning to structured answer generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase composition and II correlation.}\nWe examine whether the fraction of thinking tokens within each phase correlates with compound severity (II). At the mid-phase level, the thinking-token fraction correlates with II (Spearman $\\rho{=}0.49$, $p < 0.001$), consistent with mid-phase segments that contain more reasoning content being more vulnerable to compound sparsity. Low- and high-phase correlations are non-significant, as expected: these phases are compositionally dominated by thinking or answer tokens, respectively, leaving insufficient variance for correlation.\n\n\\paragraph{MATH-500 indirect validation.}\nDirect $\\kappa$ validation requires explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, available only on GSM8K. On MATH-500 ($n{=}500$), we instead assess \\emph{temporal ordering consistency}: the fraction of problems where the low-sparsity phase precedes the high-sparsity phase, consistent with a thinking-then-answering generation structure. DeepSeek achieves a temporal ordering score of $0.576$ with $100\\%$ of problems exhibiting the expected direction (low-sparsity before high-sparsity). Qwen, which lacks \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, shows weaker but directionally consistent temporal ordering ($0.479$). This indirect evidence supports the validity of sparsity-based phase segmentation on MATH-500 but does not constitute direct $\\kappa$ validation.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nThe direct $\\kappa$ validation ($\\kappa{=}0.88$, $n{=}48$) is limited to DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B on GSM8K; MATH-500 has only indirect (temporal ordering) validation. Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct does not produce explicit reasoning delimiters, precluding direct $\\kappa$ validation on either benchmark. Phase segmentation is computed on the dense model's attention patterns and is independent of sparsity level, so the agreement statistics are reported once rather than per-$s$.\n\n\n\\subsection{Non-Think Token Quality Under Compound Sparsity}\n\\label{app:nt-samples}\n\nTokens generated outside \\texttt{&lt;think>}...\\texttt{&lt;/think>} delimiters---i.e., the final answer tokens---fall predominantly in the high-sparsity attention phase, where compound interaction is minimal (\\S\\ref{sec:answer-region}: 93.2\\% of \\verb|\\boxed{}| markers reside in the high-sparsity phase). We illustrate the differential impact of compound sparsity on non-think (answer) versus think (reasoning) tokens with two representative examples from the GSM8K validation set (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction).\n\n\\paragraph{Example 1: Preserved answer, degraded reasoning.}\nUnder compound sparsity, the non-think answer tokens (\\texttt{The answer is 42}) are generated with near-baseline perplexity (PPL ratio $1.2{\\times}$), while the preceding think-phase tokens exhibit $4.8{\\times}$ PPL inflation. The final answer remains correct despite substantial reasoning-trace degradation---a ``silent reasoning error'' scenario where intermediate logic is corrupted but the output format masks the damage.\n\n\\paragraph{Example 2: Cascading failure at high sparsity.}\nAt $s{=}0.30$, reasoning-phase PPL inflation ($11.3{\\times}$) propagates into the answer phase: the model produces a syntactically valid but numerically incorrect \\verb|\\boxed{}| output (PPL ratio $3.1{\\times}$ on answer tokens vs.\\ $1.1{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$). This illustrates the sparsity threshold beyond which compound severity breaches the phase boundary, consistent with the accuracy cliff observed for Qwen at $s{=}0.30$ ($\\delta{=}-27$\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:accuracy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Implication.}\nThese examples illustrate that pruning-induced compound severity is phase-selective: non-think tokens are relatively protected at moderate sparsity ($s \\leq 0.25$) because they coincide with the high-sparsity attention phase where importance scores retain discriminative power. At higher sparsity ($s{=}0.30$), discrimination collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}) erodes this protection, and compound severity spills into the answer phase.\n\n\n\\section{Amplification Ratio ($G$) Details}\n\\label{app:g-metric}\n\nThe per-problem amplification ratio $G^{(i)} = \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{low}} / \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{high}}$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) provides an auxiliary illustrative perspective on the primary metric $\\Delta$. Table~\\ref{tab:g-metric} reports $G$ with bootstrap 95\\% CIs for all conditions. $G$ serves as a visualization aid; primary statistical evidence for the phase-modulated severity gradient is provided by $\\Delta$ and the associated Wilcoxon tests (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}).\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-problem amplification ratio $G$ (median of $G^{(i)}$) with bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$). $G$ serves as an auxiliary visualization of phase concentration; primary statistical evidence for the severity gradient is provided by the Interaction Index $\\Delta$ (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}). $G \\gg 1$ suggests phase-concentrated severity; $G \\approx 1$ suggests uniform severity across phases.}\n\\label{tab:g-metric}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $G$\\; [95\\% CI] (DeepSeek) & $G$\\; [95\\% CI] (Qwen) \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & $\\mathbf{7.5}$\\; $[4.1,\\; 11.4]$ & n.d.$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\n0.15 & Random     & 0.5\\; $[0.4,\\; 0.6]$ & 0.7\\; $[0.4,\\; 0.8]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & $\\mathbf{17.9}$\\; $[12.7,\\; 26.3]$ & $\\mathbf{2.6}$\\; $[0.5,\\; 5.1]$ \\\\\n0.20 & Random     & 0.8\\; $[0.6,\\; 0.9]$ & 0.9\\; $[0.7,\\; 1.0]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & $\\mathbf{49.1}$\\; $[40.1,\\; 57.8]$ & $\\mathbf{9.5}$\\; $[5.2,\\; 14.8]$ \\\\\n0.25 & Random     & 1.1\\; $[1.0,\\; 1.2]$ & 1.3\\; $[1.2,\\; 1.4]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & $\\mathbf{79.8}$\\; $[63.3,\\; 96.5]$ & $\\mathbf{36.9}$\\; $[25.3,\\; 49.1]$ \\\\\n0.30 & Random     & 1.1\\; $[1.0,\\; 1.3]$ & 1.3\\; $[1.2,\\; 1.4]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.85\\textwidth}}{\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$ $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} \\approx 0$; ratio undefined ($G$ CI: $[-0.2, 0.9]$). $\\Delta{=}0.07$, $p{=}8.7 \\times 10^{-15}$ supports $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low}} > \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nStrong phase modulation is specific to importance-based eviction ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ for DeepSeek, $2.6$--$36.9{\\times}$ for Qwen). Random eviction shows weak but statistically significant modulation at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($G \\approx 1.1$--$1.3$; CIs exclude 1), while at $s \\leq 0.20$ the CIs span or touch 1. The monotonic increase in $G$ with sparsity parallels the $\\Delta$ dose-response in Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}.\n",

```text
34	
35	\section{Multiplicative Null Verification}
36	\label{app:multiplicative-null}
37	
38	We verified that replacing the additive null $r_b = r_a + r_k - 1$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:ii}) with the multiplicative null $r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$ does not change qualitative conclusions. Under the multiplicative formulation, the interaction index becomes $\mathrm{II}_{\times} = r_b - r_a \cdot r_k$. Notably, $\mathrm{II}_{\times} < 0$ across all phases and sparsity levels: the compound interaction is \emph{sub-multiplicative} everywhere---actual compound degradation is less severe than the multiplicative prediction. $G_{\times}$ therefore quantifies the ratio of sub-multiplicative deviations between phases, not super-multiplicative amplification: a value of $G_{\times}{=}30$ means the low-sparsity phase deviates from the multiplicative null by $30{\times}$ more than the high-sparsity phase. Under importance-based eviction, $G_{\times}{=}21.1$--$36.2{\times}$ (DeepSeek) and $43.9$--$64.0{\times}$ (Qwen) across $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$, yielding a combined range of $21$--$64{\times}$. Although compound effects do not exceed the multiplicative prediction in any phase, the low-sparsity phase's deviation from the multiplicative null is $21$--$64{\times}$ larger than the high-sparsity phase's, validating that the phase-gated severity gradient is preserved under the multiplicative null. These $n{=}500$ exact values are more conservative than the preliminary $n{=}50$ calibrated estimates ($\approx$51--183${\times}$), reflecting reduced variance at full sample size. Values are computed using aggregate per-phase PPL ratios; per-problem exact computation is left for future work. Random eviction remains flat ($G_{\times} \approx 1$). The crossover at $s{=}0.25$ is preserved. We adopt the additive formulation in the main text as the more conservative baseline: it assumes zero interaction (compound loss equals the sum of individual losses), so rejection ($\mathrm{II} > 0$) establishes super-additivity regardless of null specification. The qualitative conclusions---phase-modulated severity, strategy asymmetry, and crossover---are preserved under the multiplicative null.
39	
40	% Bootstrap and permutation details moved to main text \S\ref{sec:stat-validation}
41

The crossover at $s{=}0.25$ is preserved. We adopt the additive formulation in the main text as the more conservative baseline: it assumes zero interaction (compound loss equals the sum of individual losses), so rejection ($\\mathrm{II} > 0$) establishes super-additivity regardless of null specification. The qualitative conclusions---phase-modulated severity, strategy asymmetry, and crossover---are preserved under the multiplicative null.",
          "+We verified that replacing the additive null $r_b = r_a + r_k - 1$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:ii}) with the multiplicative null $r_b = r_a \\cdot r_k$ does not change qualitative conclusions. Under the multiplicative formulation, the interaction index becomes $\\mathrm{II}_{\\times} = r_b - r_a \\cdot r_k$. Notably, $\\mathrm{II}_{\\times} < 0$ across all phases and sparsity levels: the compound interaction is \\emph{sub-multiplicative} everywhere---actual compound degradation is less severe than the multiplicative prediction. $G_{\\times}$ therefore quantifies the ratio of sub-multiplicative deviations between phases, not super-multiplicative amplification: a value of $G_{\\times}{=}30$ means the low-sparsity phase deviates from the multiplicative null by $30{\\times}$ more than the high-sparsity phase. Under importance-based eviction, $G_{\\times}{=}21.1$--$36.2{\\times}$ (DeepSeek) and $43.9$--$64.0{\\times}$ (Qwen) across $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, yielding a combined range of $21$--$64{\\times}$. Although compound effects do not exceed the multiplicative prediction in any phase, the low-sparsity phase's deviation from the multiplicative null is $21$--$64{\\times}$ larger than the high-sparsity phase's, indicating that the phase-gated severity gradient is preserved under the multiplicative null. These $n{=}500$ exact values are more conservative than the preliminary $n{=}50$ calibrated estimates ($\\approx$51--183${\\times}$), reflecting reduced variance at full sample size. Values are computed using aggregate per-phase PPL ratios; per-problem exact computation is left for future work. Random eviction remains flat ($G_{\\times} \\approx 1$). The crossover at $s{=}0.25$ is preserved. We adopt the additive formulation in the main text as the more conservative baseline: it assumes zero interaction (compound loss equals the sum of individual losses), so rejection ($\\mathrm{II} > 0$) establishes super-additivity regardless of null specification. The qualitative conclusions---phase-modulated severity, strategy asymmetry, and crossover---are preserved under the multiplicative null.",

The main analysis ($n{=}496$) appears in Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}. $G$ values in this table use the ratio-of-medians definition ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$), which differs from the primary per-problem definition in Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}. At low sparsity ($s{=}0.15$), the two definitions diverge substantially ($7.5{\\times}$ vs.\\ $69{\\times}$) because per-problem ratios are sensitive to near-zero $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$ values.}",
    "newString": "\\caption{Per-phase interaction index (II) from the $n{=}50$ profiling subset (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B). This preliminary table does not include per-condition bootstrap CIs; confirmed values with bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$) at $n{=}496$ appear in Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}, and strategy-contrast CIs for this subset appear in the permutation test (Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}). $G$ values use the ratio-of-medians definition ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) and serve as an auxiliary illustration of phase concentration; the primary evidence is $\\Delta$ (see Appendix~\\ref{app:g-metric}). At low sparsity ($s{=}0.15$), the ratio-of-medians and per-problem definitions diverge ($7.5{\\times}$ vs.\\ $69{\\times}$) because per-problem ratios are sensitive to near-zero $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$ values.}",
    "originalFile": "\n\\section{Phase-Strategy Crossover Details}\n\\label{app:crossover-details}\n\nOn DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at $s{=}0.25$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossover}): importance-based eviction yields \\emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\\ $1.113$) but \\emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$). This crossover is confirmed at all four sparsity levels, with reversal magnitude of $4.0{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$, $3.4{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, $2.6{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.25$, and $2.2{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ (decreasing as random eviction severity rises). The reversal arises because head pruning redistributes attention mass, altering importance rankings in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms in low-sparsity phases (\\S\\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}). Qwen does not exhibit a crossover: importance-based eviction produces lower II than random in both phases across all sparsity levels (e.g., at $s{=}0.25$: high $0.004$ vs.\\ $1.108$; low $1.053$ vs.\\ $1.769$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}0.25$ on DeepSeek ($n{=}496$). Grouped bar chart of median II by eviction strategy and reasoning phase. Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces $2.6{\\times}$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.}\n\\label{fig:crossover}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Profiling Subset Per-Phase Breakdown}\n\\label{app:profiling-subset}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-phase interaction index (II) from the $n{=}50$ profiling subset (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B). The main analysis ($n{=}496$) appears in Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}. $G$ values in this table use the ratio-of-medians definition ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$), which differs from the primary per-problem definition in Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}. At low sparsity ($s{=}0.15$), the two definitions diverge substantially ($7.5{\\times}$ vs.\\ $69{\\times}$) because per-problem ratios are sensitive to near-zero $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$ values.}\n\\label{tab:profiling-subset}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccccr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\Delta$ & $G$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & 0.006 & 0.081 & 0.398 & 0.39 & $\\mathbf{69}$ \\\\\n0.15 & Random     & 0.093 & 0.097 & 0.103 & 0.01 & 1.1 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & 0.015 & 0.203 & 1.496 & 1.48 & $\\mathbf{103}$ \\\\\n0.20 & Random     & 0.292 & 0.350 & 0.276 & $-$0.02 & 0.9 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & 0.032 & 1.118 & 7.196 & 7.16 & $\\mathbf{222}$ \\\\\n0.25 & Random     & 0.818 & 0.932 & 1.363 & 0.54 & 1.7 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & 0.053 & 2.283 & 14.639 & 14.59 & $\\mathbf{274}$ \\\\\n0.30 & Random     & 2.503 & 2.592 & 4.041 & 1.54 & 1.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\n\\section{Cross-Training-Paradigm Replication Details}\n\\label{app:cross-model-details}\n\nBoth models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This within-architecture-family comparison enables assessment of training methodology effects on the compound sparsity interaction, though causal attribution is limited by the observational design.\n\nDespite lacking explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{par:phase-segmentation}). Twenty-five of the 500 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}475$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\\ $n{=}496$ for DeepSeek).\n\n\\paragraph{Per-phase effective sample size.}\n\\label{app:qwen-null-rate}\nTable~\\ref{tab:qwen-effective-n} reports the per-phase effective sample size for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, defined as the number of problems with non-null phase segments (segment length $\\geq$ \\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length}). The mid-phase null rate (45.2\\%) reflects problems where mid-sparsity tokens do not form contiguous runs above the merging threshold; this does not affect the low--high contrast ($\\Delta$) used in the primary analysis.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-phase effective sample size for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\\text{eff}} = 475$). Phase segmentation is performed once on dense-model attention patterns and is independent of sparsity level.}\n\\label{tab:qwen-effective-n}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nPhase & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nHigh & 475 & 475 & 475 & 475 \\\\\nMid  & 274 & 274 & 274 & 274 \\\\\nLow  & 475 & 475 & 475 & 475 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} reports the cross-training-paradigm comparison. Qwen reproduces the monotonic severity gradient at ${\\sim}1/5$ of DeepSeek's magnitude. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross-model} visualizes this comparison.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-training-paradigm comparison of median compound severity difference ($\\Delta$) under importance-based eviction.}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}496$) & 0.39 & 1.21 & 3.99 & 8.48 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n{=}475$)         & 0.07 & 0.31 & 1.07 & 3.16 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_crossmodel.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-training-paradigm comparison of the median severity difference under importance-based eviction ($n{=}496$/$475$). Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit monotonically increasing $\\Delta$ with sparsity; Qwen's magnitude is approximately one-quarter to one-sixth of DeepSeek's.}\n\\label{fig:cross-model}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-problem concordance.}\nThe per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\\rho = -0.107$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking.\n\n\\paragraph{Random eviction control.}\nAs a control, random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$ ($\\Delta$: $-0.01$, $0.02$; CIs span zero) but significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($\\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$; $\\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $2.7$--$3.5{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based ($0.39$ vs.\\ $1.07$ at $s{=}0.25$; $0.91$ vs.\\ $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$), supporting strategy-specific amplification.\n\n\n\\section{Permutation Test Details}\n\\label{app:permutation-details}\n\nFor each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\\Delta_i^{(\\text{strat})} = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\text{median}_i\\!\\bigl(\\Delta_i^{(\\text{imp})} - \\Delta_i^{(\\text{rand})}\\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.\n\nPer-problem distributions of the paired severity difference $\\Delta_i = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under importance-based eviction are right-skewed at all sparsity levels, with mean/median ratios ranging from $1.2{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $1.6{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.30$) for DeepSeek and $1.1{\\times}$ to $1.4{\\times}$ for Qwen.\n\nThe permutation null distributions are approximately symmetric around zero at all sparsity levels. At $s{=}0.15$, the observed statistic falls at the $99.92$th percentile of the null ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} = 0.0032$); at $s \\geq 0.20$, no permutation exceeds the observed statistic.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. The monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \\to 0.69 \\to 1.66 \\to 6.70$) supports that compound severity amplification strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds. Figure~\\ref{fig:permtest-forest} presents these results as a forest plot.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired permutation test for compound severity: importance vs.\\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$ profiling subset, $10{,}000$ permutations, Bonferroni-corrected). The monotonic $\\Delta$ pattern is confirmed at $n{=}496$ (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregate-ii}).}\n\\label{tab:permutation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Observed $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ & 95\\% CI & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\\\\n0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_bootstrap.pdf}\n\\caption{Forest plot of permutation test results. Horizontal bars show 95\\% permutation CIs; diamonds mark observed median differences $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$. All CIs lie entirely above zero, supporting significant strategy-dependent severity at every sparsity level.}\n\\label{fig:permtest-forest}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Cross-Architecture Analysis}\n\\label{app:cross-arch}\n\nTo test whether the phase-modulated severity pattern extends beyond the Qwen2 architecture, we replicate the experiment on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, 8B parameters; $n{=}49$ paired problems). Table~\\ref{tab:llama-cross-arch} reports the strategy-dependent severity comparison across sparsity levels.\n\nThe evidence for a strategy contrast on LLaMA is mixed: at $s{=}0.15$, the bootstrap CI excludes zero ($[0.002, 0.043]$) but the Bonferroni-corrected Wilcoxon test is non-significant ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.219$); conversely, at $s{=}0.30$ the CI spans zero ($[-0.069, 0.544]$) while the Wilcoxon test reaches significance ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$). At intermediate levels ($s \\in \\{0.20, 0.25\\}$), neither CI nor Wilcoxon reaches significance. The median paired difference increases monotonically ($\\widetilde{\\Delta}$: $0.020 \\to 0.356$), consistent with a genuine but weak trend---providing preliminary support for the cross-architecture hypothesis while underscoring the substantially weaker interaction on LlamaForCausalLM.\\footnote{The CI/Wilcoxon discrepancy reflects differing power profiles under small $n$ with heavy-tailed distributions: the percentile bootstrap CI is based on the resampled median distribution and is less sensitive to tail observations, while the Wilcoxon signed-rank test uses signed ranks and is more sensitive to tail outliers. This inconsistency itself supports the conservative ``mixed/weak evidence'' interpretation.}\n\nThe absolute magnitude is architecture-dependent: LLaMA severity differences are ${\\sim}40{\\times}$ smaller than DeepSeek and ${\\sim}7{\\times}$ smaller than Qwen. Notably, random eviction on LLaMA also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient ($d_z$: $0.35 \\to 0.44$), substantially reducing the strategy contrast that is the hallmark of the phase-strategy interaction on Qwen2 models. This suggests that the strength of the strategy$\\times$phase interaction---not merely the severity gradient itself---is architecture-dependent. The directional pattern---importance producing larger phase gradients than random---is qualitatively consistent across all three models, but the LLaMA evidence should be interpreted as preliminary given the weak interaction magnitude and partial statistical significance.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM). $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$: median paired difference (importance $-$ random) with bootstrap 95\\% CI ($B{=}10{,}000$); $p$: Bonferroni-corrected Wilcoxon signed-rank $p$ ($k{=}4$). $n{=}49$ paired problems.}\n\\label{tab:llama-cross-arch}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$\\; [95\\% CI] & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & $0.020$\\; $[0.002,\\; 0.043]$ & $0.219$ \\\\\n0.20 & $0.022$\\; $[-0.090,\\; 0.187]$ & $0.253$ \\\\\n0.25 & $0.095$\\; $[-0.004,\\; 0.303]$ & $0.083$ \\\\\n0.30 & $0.356$\\; $[-0.069,\\; 0.544]$ & $\\mathbf{0.029}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Post-hoc power analysis.}\nWe computed post-hoc power via bootstrap simulation ($B{=}10{,}000$) to assess what claims the $n{=}49$ sample can and cannot support. For the within-importance phase gradient (testing $\\Delta_{\\text{imp}} > 0$), power exceeds $0.99$ at all four sparsity levels, indicating that the sample is sufficient to detect the importance-based severity gradient. For strategy comparisons (testing $\\Delta_{\\text{imp}} - \\Delta_{\\text{rand}} > 0$), power ranges from $0.26$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $0.63$ ($s{=}0.30$), indicating that the sample is underpowered for detecting strategy-level differences at their observed effect sizes. Extrapolating from the observed effect sizes and variance, $n \\geq 200$ paired problems would be needed to achieve $0.80$ power for the strategy comparison at $s{=}0.30$. This motivates the conservative interpretation in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}: the within-importance gradient is confirmed, but the strategy$\\times$phase interaction requires larger-scale replication.\n\n\n\\section{Analysis: Importance Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{app:analysis}\n\n\\subsection{Importance Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse}\n\nHead pruning reduces the number of heads contributing to cumulative importance scores, collapsing the score distribution. Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full} quantifies this effect: the Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas across all 16 conditions (both models $\\times$ 4 sparsity levels $\\times$ 2 strategies). The CV delta is likewise negative in all 8 importance-based conditions. The same pattern holds under random eviction, indicating that importance discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Importance discrimination collapse under importance-based eviction. $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{CV}$ are post-pruning minus baseline; negative values indicate reduced discriminative power.}\n\\label{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llrr}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{CV}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek & $0.15$ & $-0.030$ & $-0.56$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.20$ & $-0.040$ & $-0.77$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.25$ & $-0.065$ & $-1.29$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.30$ & $-0.081$ & $-1.66$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen & $0.15$ & $-0.010$ & $-0.05$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.20$ & $-0.012$ & $-0.21$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.25$ & $-0.010$ & $-1.49$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.30$ & $-0.020$ & $-1.14$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nWhen importance scores lose discriminability, thresholding degenerates into noise-sensitive selection---functionally approaching biased random eviction. This is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced collapse has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. Random eviction is immune because its decisions are independent of score quality, explaining $G \\approx 1$.\n\n\\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}\n\\label{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n\nBeyond importance discrimination collapse, we examine whether head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads. We measure the mean attention entropy per head:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\mathcal{H}_i^{(h)} = -\\sum_{j=1}^{T} a_{ij}^{(h)} \\log a_{ij}^{(h)}\n    \\label{eq:attn-entropy}\n\\end{equation}\nand compute $\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$ as the change from baseline (no pruning) to the pruned condition.\n\nFor DeepSeek under importance-based eviction, the entropy delta is consistently negative across all four sparsity levels ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}), indicating that importance-based pruning systematically selects heads whose removal concentrates attention in the surviving heads, reinforcing importance discrimination collapse.\n\nFor Qwen, the entropy pattern only partially replicates. The delta is negative at $s{=}0.15$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = -0.073$) and $s{=}0.20$ ($-0.076$), consistent with DeepSeek. However, at $s{=}0.25$, the delta reverses to $+0.045$, with 22 of 30 problems showing positive entropy change. At $s{=}0.30$, the delta is near zero ($-0.007$). The $s{=}0.25$ reversal is systematic rather than a statistical artifact: it is consistent across all three reasoning phases (low: $+0.042$, mid: $+0.034$, high: $+0.017$). We interpret this as a genuine model-specific difference, possibly arising from distinct head specialization patterns: Qwen's instruction tuning (vs.\\ DeepSeek's distillation-based training) may produce head configurations where removing 25\\% of heads triggers compensatory broadening rather than concentration.\n\nWe characterize importance discrimination collapse as the robust, model-independent driver of the phase-strategy interaction, with entropy redistribution as a secondary amplifier that is consistent in DeepSeek but model-dependent in Qwen.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Attention entropy deltas ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$) under importance-based eviction. DeepSeek shows consistent entropy compression; Qwen partially replicates with a reversal at $s{=}0.25$.}\n\\label{tab:entropy-deltas}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llr}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & $\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek & $0.15$ & $-0.040$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.20$ & $-0.047$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.25$ & $-0.045$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.30$ & $-0.044$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen & $0.15$ & $-0.073$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.20$ & $-0.076$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.25$ & $+0.045$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.30$ & $-0.007$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",

The main analysis ($n{=}496$) appears in Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}. $G$ values in this table use the ratio-of-medians definition ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$), which differs from the primary per-problem definition in Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}. At low sparsity ($s{=}0.15$), the two definitions diverge substantially ($7.5{\\times}$ vs.\\ $69{\\times}$) because per-problem ratios are sensitive to near-zero $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$ values.}",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Importance Discrimination Collapse Details}\n\\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}\n\nThis cross-model consistency (Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) supports importance discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$), indicating that importance discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended-discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}\nThe compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \\leq 0.30$).\n\n\\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \\approx 0.20$: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$ for both models, but significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (DS: $\\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$; QW: $\\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $2.7$--$17{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based (restricted to conditions where both strategies are significant, $s \\geq 0.25$), supporting that the dominant amplification is strategy-specific.\n\n\\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}\nDirectly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.\n\n\n\\section{Pilot Mitigation Experiment}\n\\label{app:pilot-mitigation}\n\nWe explored oracle phase-aware budget reallocation, shifting sparsity budget from high-severity low-sparsity phases to low-severity high-sparsity phases ($n{=}30$ problems at $s{=}0.25$). Oracle recovery---the fraction of compound interaction eliminated by phase-aware allocation---reached 18\\% (95\\% CI: $[-39\\%, 45.8\\%]$; Wilcoxon $p = 0.002$), below the pre-registered 30\\% gate for claiming practical mitigation. A heuristic variant achieved 32\\% recovery (95\\% CI: $[-22.8\\%, 66\\%]$; $p = 0.024$). \\textbf{Critically, 95\\% bootstrap CIs for both conditions span zero}, meaning neither recovery estimate is statistically distinguishable from no improvement. \\textbf{Gate status: FAIL}---neither oracle nor heuristic recovery meets the pre-registered threshold, and neither is statistically distinguishable from zero improvement. These results indicate that phase-aware budget reallocation alone is insufficient to neutralize the severity gradient; effective mitigation likely requires joint optimization of budget allocation and eviction strategy selection across phases.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.\nWhether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.\nLarger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the importance discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).\n\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-training-paradigm replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nPilot cross-architecture evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-arch}) suggests qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \\sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This preliminary evidence ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) is consistent with the qualitative pattern extending beyond Qwen2 but is insufficient for definitive cross-architecture claims; quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (seed$=$42). The primary analysis uses $n{=}496$ paired problems for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded); the permutation test (Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}) uses $n{=}49$ from an initial profiling subset.\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratios up to $43{:}1$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives.\nAll four profiling-subset permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---supporting the effect direction at every sparsity level. At $n{=}496$, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests support all conditions with $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification.\n\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction on both models becomes significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (Qwen: $\\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ at $s{=}0.25$; $\\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ at $s{=}0.30$; bootstrap CIs exclude zero).\nThese effects are genuine but $2.7$--$20{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based eviction at the same sparsity levels, consistent with a weak phase gradient that is detectable at sufficient sample size but not the primary driver of compound severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur primary analysis spans $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$; pilot data at $s{=}0.10$ and $s{=}0.35$ exist (6 total sparsity levels) but are not included in the main analysis due to limited sample size at these boundary conditions. Monotonicity is established at the tested sparsity levels (JT test $p \\ll 10^{-10}$); the functional form of $G(s)$ between and beyond these levels is not characterized.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\n\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n\n\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{app:gsm8k}\n\nTables~\\ref{tab:gsm8k-ds} and \\ref{tab:gsm8k-qw} report the per-phase II breakdown on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) for DeepSeek and Qwen, respectively. All statistics use medians, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests, and bootstrap median CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$), matching the MATH-500 methodology. DeepSeek importance-based eviction replicates the MATH-500 phase gradient across all four sparsity levels (all $p \\leq 0.0003$) with a strict dose-response ($\\Delta$: $0.24 \\to 4.68$). Qwen importance-based eviction is significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($p < 10^{-4}$). Random eviction is non-significant across all conditions for both models; Qwen random $s{=}0.30$ is excluded due to data quality concerns (output truncation producing extreme II values).\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-phase interaction index on GSM8K: DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}50$). $\\Delta$: paired median difference ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) with bootstrap 95\\% CI ($B{=}10{,}000$, median statistic). $p$: Wilcoxon signed-rank, Bonferroni-corrected ($k{=}4$).}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k-ds}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\Delta$\\; [95\\% CI] & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & $-$0.001 & 0.241 & 0.24\\; $[0.08,\\; 0.48]$ & 0.0003 \\\\\n0.15 & Random     & 0.036 & 0.105 & $-$0.04\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.90 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & $-$0.002 & 0.740 & 0.74\\; $[0.31,\\; 1.12]$ & ${<}\\,10^{-4}$ \\\\\n0.20 & Random     & 0.143 & 0.423 & 0.28\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.96 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & 0.009 & 1.992 & 1.99\\; $[0.93,\\; 2.82]$ & ${<}\\,10^{-4}$ \\\\\n0.25 & Random     & 0.619 & 1.603 & 0.98\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.83 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & 0.024 & 4.682 & 4.68\\; $[2.12,\\; 10.56]$ & ${<}\\,10^{-4}$ \\\\\n0.30 & Random     & 1.837 & 4.683 & 2.85\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.54 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-phase interaction index on GSM8K: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n{=}50$). Outputs truncated at \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}${=}$2047; absolute values should be interpreted with this limitation. $^{\\ddagger}$Excluded: extreme II values from truncation artifacts render this condition invalid.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k-qw}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\Delta$\\; [95\\% CI] & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & 0.000 & 0.029 & 0.03\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.39 \\\\\n0.15 & Random     & 0.089 & 0.033 & $-$1.58\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.69 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & 0.004 & 0.180 & 0.09\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.62 \\\\\n0.20 & Random     & 0.201 & 0.356 & 4.88\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.57 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & 0.009 & 1.064 & 1.14\\; $[0.62,\\; 1.66]$ & ${<}\\,10^{-4}$ \\\\\n0.25 & Random     & 0.802 & 1.213 & 11.1\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.39 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & 0.023 & 2.140 & 4.62\\; $[2.88,\\; 6.92]$ & ${<}\\,10^{-4}$ \\\\\n0.30 & Random$^{\\ddagger}$     & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textit{excluded (data quality)}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\n\\section{Downstream Accuracy Validation Details}\n\\label{app:accuracy-details}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:accuracy} reports MATH-500 accuracy under compound sparsity (importance-based eviction) at $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$ with \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}${=}$8192, complementing the PPL-based analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:accuracy-validation}. Qwen ($n{=}100$; truncation $\\leq 4\\%$ across all conditions) provides the cleanest dose-response evidence: accuracy declines gradually from $46\\%$ (dense) through $s{=}0.25$ ($\\delta{=}-4$ to $-13$\\,pp) before a sharp cliff at $s{=}0.30$ ($19\\%$, $\\delta{=}-27$\\,pp). DeepSeek ($n{=}50$) shows steeper overall degradation ($74\\% \\to 4\\%$), confounded by rising truncation ($16\\% \\to 76\\%$); non-truncated analysis (\\S\\ref{app:accuracy-nt}) reveals a dual-regime structure.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{MATH-500 accuracy under compound sparsity (importance-based eviction, \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}${=}$8192). $\\delta$: accuracy change vs.\\ dense. Trunc: truncation rate under sparse condition. DS: $n{=}50$ (dense truncation $16\\%$); QW: $n{=}100$ (truncation $\\leq 4\\%$ all conditions).}\n\\label{tab:accuracy}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & Dense & Sparse & $\\delta$ & Trunc \\\\\n\\midrule\nDS & 0.15 & 74\\% & 48\\% & $-$26\\,pp & 32\\% \\\\\nDS & 0.20 & 74\\% & 30\\% & $-$44\\,pp & 56\\% \\\\\nDS & 0.25 & 74\\% & 8\\% & $-$66\\,pp & 76\\% \\\\\nDS & 0.30 & 74\\% & 4\\% & $-$70\\,pp & 76\\% \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQW & 0.15 & 46\\% & 42\\% & $-$4\\,pp & $\\leq$4\\% \\\\\nQW & 0.20 & 46\\% & 36\\% & $-$10\\,pp & $\\leq$4\\% \\\\\nQW & 0.25 & 46\\% & 33\\% & $-$13\\,pp & $\\leq$4\\% \\\\\nQW & 0.30 & 46\\% & 19\\% & $-$27\\,pp & $\\leq$4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Failure mode divergence.}\nDeepSeek and Qwen exhibit qualitatively distinct failure modes under compound sparsity. DeepSeek failures are \\emph{truncation-dominated}: compound sparsity is associated with verbose, repetitive chain-of-thought that exceeds the token limit (truncation rises from $16\\%$ at dense to $32\\%$ at $s{=}0.15$ and $76\\%$ at $s{=}0.30$, even with \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}${=}$8192). Qwen failures are \\emph{wrong-answer-dominated}: outputs complete within the token limit (truncation $\\leq 4\\%$) but produce incorrect answers, indicating that compound sparsity disrupts reasoning quality directly. This divergence likely reflects training-paradigm differences: DeepSeek's distillation from DeepSeek-R1 produces extended CoT traces susceptible to truncation under degraded conditions.\n\n\\paragraph{Language switching under high sparsity.}\nDeepSeek problems exhibit language switching to Chinese mid-generation at the following observed rates: 0\\% at dense, 4\\% at $s{=}0.15$, and 40\\% at $s{=}0.30$ (intermediate sparsity levels not yet measured).\\footnote{Language switching rates were detected from \\texttt{gen\\_snippet} fields in 2048-token evaluations, not from the independent 8192-token generations used in Table~\\ref{tab:accuracy}. Rates under the 8192-token budget were not re-measured.} This qualitative pathology---absent in Qwen---is a \\emph{symptom} of compound-sparsity-induced degradation rather than a cause of accuracy loss: at $s{=}0.30$, Chinese-language and English-language outputs achieve comparable accuracy ($\\sim$10\\%), indicating that the accuracy decline reflects pruning-induced reasoning corruption regardless of output language. The switching pattern is consistent with the model's Chinese-language pretraining corpus surfacing under degraded attention in distillation-trained models.\n\n\n\\section{Non-Truncated Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:accuracy-nt}\n\nWith \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}${=}$8192, Qwen truncation remains $\\leq 4\\%$ across all sparsity levels, so the full-sample accuracy decline ($\\delta{=}-4$ to $-27$\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:accuracy}) reflects pure reasoning quality degradation without truncation confound.\n\nDeepSeek exhibits substantial truncation ($16\\%$ at dense to $76\\%$ at $s{=}0.25$--$0.30$). Restricting to non-truncated outputs reveals a \\emph{dual-regime} structure. At $s \\leq 0.20$, non-truncated accuracy remains $68$--$71\\%$ (vs.\\ $88\\%$ dense), while full-sample accuracy drops to $30$--$48\\%$---indicating that degradation at low sparsity is primarily truncation-driven rather than reflecting reasoning quality loss. At $s \\geq 0.25$, non-truncated accuracy itself collapses: $33\\%$ at $s{=}0.25$ and $17\\%$ at $s{=}0.30$ ($n_{\\text{NT}}{=}12$ for both; reported as reference values given small samples), showing that high-dose compound sparsity degrades both generation length \\emph{and} reasoning quality simultaneously. The NT accuracy cliff from $68\\%$ ($s{=}0.20$) to $33\\%$ ($s{=}0.25$) localizes the transition between truncation-driven and dual-collapse regimes.\n\nThis dual-regime structure complements the importance discrimination collapse analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}): at low sparsity, compound interaction primarily manifests as increased verbosity (degraded attention produces repetitive CoT); at high sparsity, the interaction additionally corrupts reasoning-critical computations.\n",

          " Tables~\\ref{tab:gsm8k-ds} and \\ref{tab:gsm8k-qw} report the per-phase II breakdown on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) for DeepSeek and Qwen, respectively. All statistics use medians, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests, and bootstrap median CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$), matching the MATH-500 methodology. DeepSeek importance-based eviction replicates the MATH-500 phase gradient across all four sparsity levels (all $p \\leq 0.0003$) with a strict dose-response ($\\Delta$: $0.24 \\to 4.68$). Qwen importance-based eviction is significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($p < 10^{-4}$). Random eviction is non-significant across all conditions for both models; Qwen random $s{=}0.30$ is excluded due to data quality concerns (output truncation producing extreme II values).",

All 8 random conditions (2 models $\\times$ 4 sparsity levels) show $|d| \\leq 0.164$, well below the detectability threshold; their non-significance should therefore be interpreted as absence of evidence rather than evidence of absence. By contrast, Qwen importance-based eviction at $s \\geq 0.25$ is adequately powered ($d \\approx 0.63$, power ${>}\\,0.99$), confirming that the observed significance reflects a genuine effect.",
