    "newString": "\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe $n{=}50$ profiling subset served as a preliminary calibration; all importance-based $\\Delta$ values were subsequently confirmed at $n{=}496$ with bootstrap 95\\% CIs excluding zero (Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}: $\\Delta{=}0.39$\\;$[0.35,\\;0.44]$ to $8.48$\\;$[7.75,\\;9.69]$). The paired permutation test on this subset ($n{=}49$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}) provides bootstrap 95\\% CIs for the strategy contrast ($\\widetilde{\\Delta}_{\\text{imp}} - \\widetilde{\\Delta}_{\\text{rand}}$), all excluding zero after Bonferroni correction.\n\n\n\\section{Cross-Training-Paradigm Replication Details}",
    "originalFile": "\n\\section{Phase-Strategy Crossover Details}\n\\label{app:crossover-details}\n\nOn DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at $s{=}0.25$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossover}): importance-based eviction yields \\emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\\ $1.113$) but \\emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$). This crossover is confirmed at all four sparsity levels, with reversal magnitude of $4.0{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$, $3.4{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, $2.6{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.25$, and $2.2{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ (decreasing as random eviction severity rises). The reversal arises because head pruning redistributes attention mass, altering importance rankings in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms in low-sparsity phases (\\S\\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}). Qwen does not exhibit a crossover: importance-based eviction produces lower II than random in both phases across all sparsity levels (e.g., at $s{=}0.25$: high $0.004$ vs.\\ $1.108$; low $1.053$ vs.\\ $1.769$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}0.25$ on DeepSeek ($n{=}496$). Grouped bar chart of median II by eviction strategy and reasoning phase. Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces $2.6{\\times}$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.}\n\\label{fig:crossover}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Profiling Subset Per-Phase Breakdown}\n\\label{app:profiling-subset}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-phase interaction index (II) from the $n{=}50$ profiling subset (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B). This preliminary table does not include per-condition bootstrap CIs; confirmed values with bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$) at $n{=}496$ appear in Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}, and strategy-contrast CIs for this subset appear in the permutation test (Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}). $G$ values use the ratio-of-medians definition ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) and serve as an auxiliary illustration of phase concentration; the primary evidence is $\\Delta$ (see Appendix~\\ref{app:g-metric}). At low sparsity ($s{=}0.15$), the ratio-of-medians and per-problem definitions diverge ($7.5{\\times}$ vs.\\ $69{\\times}$) because per-problem ratios are sensitive to near-zero $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$ values.}\n\\label{tab:profiling-subset}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccccr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\Delta$ & $G$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & 0.006 & 0.081 & 0.398 & 0.39 & $\\mathbf{69}$ \\\\\n0.15 & Random     & 0.093 & 0.097 & 0.103 & 0.01 & 1.1 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & 0.015 & 0.203 & 1.496 & 1.48 & $\\mathbf{103}$ \\\\\n0.20 & Random     & 0.292 & 0.350 & 0.276 & $-$0.02 & 0.9 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & 0.032 & 1.118 & 7.196 & 7.16 & $\\mathbf{222}$ \\\\\n0.25 & Random     & 0.818 & 0.932 & 1.363 & 0.54 & 1.7 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & 0.053 & 2.283 & 14.639 & 14.59 & $\\mathbf{274}$ \\\\\n0.30 & Random     & 2.503 & 2.592 & 4.041 & 1.54 & 1.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\n\\section{Cross-Training-Paradigm Replication Details}\n\\label{app:cross-model-details}\n\nBoth models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This within-architecture-family comparison enables assessment of training methodology effects on the compound sparsity interaction, though causal attribution is limited by the observational design.\n\nDespite lacking explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{par:phase-segmentation}). Twenty-five of the 500 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}475$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\\ $n{=}496$ for DeepSeek).\n\n\\paragraph{Per-phase effective sample size.}\n\\label{app:qwen-null-rate}\nTable~\\ref{tab:qwen-effective-n} reports the per-phase effective sample size for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, defined as the number of problems with non-null phase segments (segment length $\\geq$ \\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length}). The mid-phase null rate (45.2\\%) reflects problems where mid-sparsity tokens do not form contiguous runs above the merging threshold; this does not affect the low--high contrast ($\\Delta$) used in the primary analysis.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-phase effective sample size for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\\text{eff}} = 475$). Phase segmentation is performed once on dense-model attention patterns and is independent of sparsity level.}\n\\label{tab:qwen-effective-n}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nPhase & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nHigh & 475 & 475 & 475 & 475 \\\\\nMid  & 274 & 274 & 274 & 274 \\\\\nLow  & 475 & 475 & 475 & 475 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} reports the cross-training-paradigm comparison. Qwen reproduces the monotonic severity gradient at ${\\sim}1/5$ of DeepSeek's magnitude. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross-model} visualizes this comparison.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-training-paradigm comparison of median compound severity difference ($\\Delta$) under importance-based eviction.}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}496$) & 0.39 & 1.21 & 3.99 & 8.48 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n{=}475$)         & 0.07 & 0.31 & 1.07 & 3.16 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_crossmodel.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-training-paradigm comparison of the median severity difference under importance-based eviction ($n{=}496$/$475$). Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit monotonically increasing $\\Delta$ with sparsity; Qwen's magnitude is approximately one-quarter to one-sixth of DeepSeek's.}\n\\label{fig:cross-model}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-problem concordance.}\nThe per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\\rho = -0.107$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking.\n\n\\paragraph{Random eviction control.}\nAs a control, random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$ ($\\Delta$: $-0.01$, $0.02$; CIs span zero) but significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($\\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$; $\\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $2.7$--$3.5{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based ($0.39$ vs.\\ $1.07$ at $s{=}0.25$; $0.91$ vs.\\ $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$), supporting strategy-specific amplification.\n\n\n\\section{Permutation Test Details}\n\\label{app:permutation-details}\n\nFor each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\\Delta_i^{(\\text{strat})} = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\text{median}_i\\!\\bigl(\\Delta_i^{(\\text{imp})} - \\Delta_i^{(\\text{rand})}\\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.\n\nPer-problem distributions of the paired severity difference $\\Delta_i = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under importance-based eviction are right-skewed at all sparsity levels, with mean/median ratios ranging from $1.2{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $1.6{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.30$) for DeepSeek and $1.1{\\times}$ to $1.4{\\times}$ for Qwen.\n\nThe permutation null distributions are approximately symmetric around zero at all sparsity levels. At $s{=}0.15$, the observed statistic falls at the $99.92$th percentile of the null ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} = 0.0032$); at $s \\geq 0.20$, no permutation exceeds the observed statistic.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. The monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \\to 0.69 \\to 1.66 \\to 6.70$) supports that compound severity amplification strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds. Figure~\\ref{fig:permtest-forest} presents these results as a forest plot.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired permutation test for compound severity: importance vs.\\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$ profiling subset, $10{,}000$ permutations, Bonferroni-corrected). The monotonic $\\Delta$ pattern is confirmed at $n{=}496$ (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregate-ii}).}\n\\label{tab:permutation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Observed $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ & 95\\% CI & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\\\\n0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_bootstrap.pdf}\n\\caption{Forest plot of permutation test results. Horizontal bars show 95\\% permutation CIs; diamonds mark observed median differences $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$. All CIs lie entirely above zero, supporting significant strategy-dependent severity at every sparsity level.}\n\\label{fig:permtest-forest}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Cross-Architecture Analysis}\n\\label{app:cross-arch}\n\nTo test whether the phase-modulated severity pattern extends beyond the Qwen2 architecture, we replicate the experiment on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, 8B parameters; $n{=}49$ paired problems). Table~\\ref{tab:llama-cross-arch} reports the strategy-dependent severity comparison across sparsity levels.\n\nThe evidence for a strategy contrast on LLaMA is mixed: at $s{=}0.15$, the bootstrap CI excludes zero ($[0.002, 0.043]$) but the Bonferroni-corrected Wilcoxon test is non-significant ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.219$); conversely, at $s{=}0.30$ the CI spans zero ($[-0.069, 0.544]$) while the Wilcoxon test reaches significance ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$). At intermediate levels ($s \\in \\{0.20, 0.25\\}$), neither CI nor Wilcoxon reaches significance. The median paired difference increases monotonically ($\\widetilde{\\Delta}$: $0.020 \\to 0.356$), consistent with a genuine but weak trend---providing preliminary support for the cross-architecture hypothesis while underscoring the substantially weaker interaction on LlamaForCausalLM.\\footnote{The CI/Wilcoxon discrepancy reflects differing power profiles under small $n$ with heavy-tailed distributions: the percentile bootstrap CI is based on the resampled median distribution and is less sensitive to tail observations, while the Wilcoxon signed-rank test uses signed ranks and is more sensitive to tail outliers. This inconsistency itself supports the conservative ``mixed/weak evidence'' interpretation.}\n\nThe absolute magnitude is architecture-dependent: LLaMA severity differences are ${\\sim}40{\\times}$ smaller than DeepSeek and ${\\sim}7{\\times}$ smaller than Qwen. Notably, random eviction on LLaMA also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient ($d_z$: $0.35 \\to 0.44$), substantially reducing the strategy contrast that is the hallmark of the phase-strategy interaction on Qwen2 models. This suggests that the strength of the strategy$\\times$phase interaction---not merely the severity gradient itself---is architecture-dependent. The directional pattern---importance producing larger phase gradients than random---is qualitatively consistent across all three models, but the LLaMA evidence should be interpreted as preliminary given the weak interaction magnitude and partial statistical significance.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM). $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$: median paired difference (importance $-$ random) with bootstrap 95\\% CI ($B{=}10{,}000$); $p$: Bonferroni-corrected Wilcoxon signed-rank $p$ ($k{=}4$). $n{=}49$ paired problems.}\n\\label{tab:llama-cross-arch}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$\\; [95\\% CI] & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & $0.020$\\; $[0.002,\\; 0.043]$ & $0.219$ \\\\\n0.20 & $0.022$\\; $[-0.090,\\; 0.187]$ & $0.253$ \\\\\n0.25 & $0.095$\\; $[-0.004,\\; 0.303]$ & $0.083$ \\\\\n0.30 & $0.356$\\; $[-0.069,\\; 0.544]$ & $\\mathbf{0.029}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Post-hoc power analysis.}\nWe computed post-hoc power via bootstrap simulation ($B{=}10{,}000$) to assess what claims the $n{=}49$ sample can and cannot support. For the within-importance phase gradient (testing $\\Delta_{\\text{imp}} > 0$), power exceeds $0.99$ at all four sparsity levels, indicating that the sample is sufficient to detect the importance-based severity gradient. For strategy comparisons (testing $\\Delta_{\\text{imp}} - \\Delta_{\\text{rand}} > 0$), power ranges from $0.26$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $0.63$ ($s{=}0.30$), indicating that the sample is underpowered for detecting strategy-level differences at their observed effect sizes. Extrapolating from the observed effect sizes and variance, $n \\geq 200$ paired problems would be needed to achieve $0.80$ power for the strategy comparison at $s{=}0.30$. This motivates the conservative interpretation in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}: the within-importance gradient is confirmed, but the strategy$\\times$phase interaction requires larger-scale replication.\n\n\n\\section{Analysis: Importance Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{app:analysis}\n\n\\subsection{Importance Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse}\n\nHead pruning reduces the number of heads contributing to cumulative importance scores, collapsing the score distribution. Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full} quantifies this effect: the Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas across all 16 conditions (both models $\\times$ 4 sparsity levels $\\times$ 2 strategies). The CV delta is likewise negative in all 8 importance-based conditions. The same pattern holds under random eviction, indicating that importance discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Importance discrimination collapse under importance-based eviction. $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{CV}$ are post-pruning minus baseline; negative values indicate reduced discriminative power.}\n\\label{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llrr}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{CV}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek & $0.15$ & $-0.030$ & $-0.56$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.20$ & $-0.040$ & $-0.77$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.25$ & $-0.065$ & $-1.29$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.30$ & $-0.081$ & $-1.66$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen & $0.15$ & $-0.010$ & $-0.05$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.20$ & $-0.012$ & $-0.21$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.25$ & $-0.010$ & $-1.49$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.30$ & $-0.020$ & $-1.14$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nWhen importance scores lose discriminability, thresholding degenerates into noise-sensitive selection---functionally approaching biased random eviction. This is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced collapse has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. Random eviction is immune because its decisions are independent of score quality, explaining $G \\approx 1$.\n\n\\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}\n\\label{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n\nBeyond importance discrimination collapse, we examine whether head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads. We measure the mean attention entropy per head:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\mathcal{H}_i^{(h)} = -\\sum_{j=1}^{T} a_{ij}^{(h)} \\log a_{ij}^{(h)}\n    \\label{eq:attn-entropy}\n\\end{equation}\nand compute $\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$ as the change from baseline (no pruning) to the pruned condition.\n\nFor DeepSeek under importance-based eviction, the entropy delta is consistently negative across all four sparsity levels ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}), indicating that importance-based pruning systematically selects heads whose removal concentrates attention in the surviving heads, reinforcing importance discrimination collapse.\n\nFor Qwen, the entropy pattern only partially replicates. The delta is negative at $s{=}0.15$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = -0.073$) and $s{=}0.20$ ($-0.076$), consistent with DeepSeek. However, at $s{=}0.25$, the delta reverses to $+0.045$, with 22 of 30 problems showing positive entropy change. At $s{=}0.30$, the delta is near zero ($-0.007$). The $s{=}0.25$ reversal is systematic rather than a statistical artifact: it is consistent across all three reasoning phases (low: $+0.042$, mid: $+0.034$, high: $+0.017$). We interpret this as a genuine model-specific difference, possibly arising from distinct head specialization patterns: Qwen's instruction tuning (vs.\\ DeepSeek's distillation-based training) may produce head configurations where removing 25\\% of heads triggers compensatory broadening rather than concentration.\n\nWe characterize importance discrimination collapse as the robust, model-independent driver of the phase-strategy interaction, with entropy redistribution as a secondary amplifier that is consistent in DeepSeek but model-dependent in Qwen.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Attention entropy deltas ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$) under importance-based eviction. DeepSeek shows consistent entropy compression; Qwen partially replicates with a reversal at $s{=}0.25$.}\n\\label{tab:entropy-deltas}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llr}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & $\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek & $0.15$ & $-0.040$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.20$ & $-0.047$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.25$ & $-0.045$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.30$ & $-0.044$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen & $0.15$ & $-0.073$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.20$ & $-0.076$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.25$ & $+0.045$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.30$ & $-0.007$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",

20	\section{Pilot Mitigation Experiment}
21	\label{app:pilot-mitigation}
22	
23	We explored oracle phase-aware budget reallocation, shifting sparsity budget from high-severity low-sparsity phases to low-severity high-sparsity phases ($n{=}30$ problems at $s{=}0.25$). Oracle recovery---the fraction of compound interaction eliminated by phase-aware allocation---reached 18\% (95\% CI: $[-39\%, 45.8\%]$; Wilcoxon $p = 0.002$), below the pre-registered 30\% gate for claiming practical mitigation. A heuristic variant achieved 32\% recovery (95\% CI: $[-22.8\%, 66\%]$; $p = 0.024$). \textbf{Critically, 95\% bootstrap CIs for both conditions span zero}, meaning neither recovery estimate is statistically distinguishable from no improvement. \textbf{Gate status: FAIL}---neither oracle nor heuristic recovery meets the pre-registered threshold, and neither is statistically distinguishable from zero improvement. These results indicate that phase-aware budget reallocation alone is insufficient to neutralize the severity gradient; effective mitigation likely requires joint optimization of budget allocation and eviction strategy selection across phases.
24	
25	
26	\section{Extended Limitations}
27	\label{app:limitations}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013iQxUeqmVoLK5QdaXEEWxv`</summary>

```text
19	$\mathrm{II} > 0$ indicates super-additive degradation; $\mathrm{II} = 0$ indicates additive independence. We adopt the additive null as primary: it assumes $r_b = r_a + r_k - 1$ (compound loss equals sum of individual losses), the most conservative independence assumption. Although the two axes are mechanistically linked (\S\ref{sec:analysis}), rejection ($\mathrm{II} > 0$) establishes super-additivity regardless of null specification. Appendix~\ref{app:multiplicative-null} reports parallel results under the multiplicative null ($r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$); the phase-severity gradient is preserved ($G_{\times}{=}21$--$64{\times}$), supporting null-choice invariance. The II distribution is heavily right-skewed; we report medians throughout.
20	
21	\paragraph{Severity gradient.}
22	To capture phase variation, the per-problem \emph{amplification ratio} is:
23	\begin{equation}
24	    G^{(i)} = \frac{\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\,\textit{low}}}{\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\,\textit{high}}},
25	    \label{eq:gradient}
26	\end{equation}
27	where $\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\,\textit{low/high}}$ is the interaction index in the low/high-sparsity phase for problem $i$. We report $G = \widetilde{G^{(i)}}$, the median across problems, with bootstrap CIs. We use the median of per-problem ratios (not the ratio of medians) to preserve per-problem pairing; see Appendix~\ref{app:profiling-subset} for comparison. $G \gg 1$ indicates phase-concentrated severity.
28	
29	\paragraph{Phase segmentation.}
30	\label{par:phase-segmentation}
31	Inspired by ThinKV's phase-aware perspective on reasoning-time compression~\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}, each generated sequence is partitioned into three phases (low-, mid-, high-sparsity) by tercile boundaries of per-position attention sparsity, using a quantile-based segmentation rather than explicit reasoning delimiters (e.g., \texttt{&lt;think>} tokens). Low-sparsity phases correspond to broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning); high-sparsity phases to concentrated attention (structured computation). We note that this segmentation is an \emph{operationalization choice}: attention sparsity serves as a continuous proxy for reasoning phase rather than a theoretically grounded boundary. We validate robustness to segmentation granularity (tercile/quartile/quintile; Appendix~\ref{app:seg-robust}) and minimum phase length (Appendix~\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}). Validation against DeepSeek's explicit \texttt{&lt;think>} markers using adaptive per-sequence quantile segmentation yields substantial agreement ($\kappa{=}0.88$, AUC-ROC${=}0.98$; $n{=}48$; Appendix~\ref{app:think-validation}). Details in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}.
32	
```

    "newString": "where $\\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\,\\textit{low/high}}$ is the interaction index in the low/high-sparsity phase for problem $i$. We report $G = \\widetilde{G^{(i)}}$, the median across problems, with bootstrap CIs. We use the median of per-problem ratios (not the ratio of medians) to preserve per-problem pairing; see Appendix~\\ref{app:profiling-subset} for comparison. $G$ serves as an auxiliary descriptive ratio that illustrates the degree of phase concentration; the primary statistical evidence for the severity gradient rests on $\\Delta$ and paired Wilcoxon tests (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}).",
    "originalFile": "% Section 3: Background and Problem Setup\n% Note: eq:ii and eq:gradient are the canonical definitions.\n% Full formulation details in Appendix (app:formulation-details).\n\n\\section{Background and Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:background}\n\nWe formalize compound sparsity, define the Interaction Index, and introduce phase segmentation. Full mathematical details appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:formulation-details}.\n\n\\paragraph{Compound sparsity.}\nLet $s \\in (0,1)$ denote a unified sparsity budget. \\emph{Attention head pruning} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of heads per layer by Frobenius norm importance \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}. \\emph{KV cache eviction} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of token positions from the cache. We compare two strategies: \\emph{importance-based}, which scores positions by position-normalized cumulative attention mass (Eq.~\\ref{eq:kv-importance} in Appendix~\\ref{app:formulation-details}), and \\emph{random}, which evicts uniformly at random \\cite{nacl-acl2024}. Applying both simultaneously yields \\emph{compound sparsity}; the two axes interact because head pruning alters the importance ranking used for eviction \\cite{spark-aaai2026}. Existing joint-pruning approaches such as GRASPrune \\cite{graspune-acl2026} learn global budget-constrained gates but treat decisions as static across generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Interaction Index.}\nFor a text segment, let $\\text{PPL}_c$ denote perplexity under condition $c \\in \\{d, a, k, b\\}$ (dense, attention-only, KV-only, compound). Normalizing by the dense baseline yields $r_c = \\text{PPL}_c / \\text{PPL}_d$, and:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\mathrm{II} = r_b - r_a - r_k + 1.\n    \\label{eq:ii}\n\\end{equation}\n$\\mathrm{II} > 0$ indicates super-additive degradation; $\\mathrm{II} = 0$ indicates additive independence. We adopt the additive null as primary: it assumes $r_b = r_a + r_k - 1$ (compound loss equals sum of individual losses), the most conservative independence assumption. Although the two axes are mechanistically linked (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}), rejection ($\\mathrm{II} > 0$) establishes super-additivity regardless of null specification. Appendix~\\ref{app:multiplicative-null} reports parallel results under the multiplicative null ($r_b = r_a \\cdot r_k$); the phase-severity gradient is preserved ($G_{\\times}{=}21$--$64{\\times}$), supporting null-choice invariance. The II distribution is heavily right-skewed; we report medians throughout.\n\n\\paragraph{Severity gradient.}\nTo capture phase variation, the per-problem \\emph{amplification ratio} is:\n\\begin{equation}\n    G^{(i)} = \\frac{\\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\,\\textit{low}}}{\\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\,\\textit{high}}},\n    \\label{eq:gradient}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\,\\textit{low/high}}$ is the interaction index in the low/high-sparsity phase for problem $i$. We report $G = \\widetilde{G^{(i)}}$, the median across problems, with bootstrap CIs. We use the median of per-problem ratios (not the ratio of medians) to preserve per-problem pairing; see Appendix~\\ref{app:profiling-subset} for comparison. $G \\gg 1$ indicates phase-concentrated severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase segmentation.}\n\\label{par:phase-segmentation}\nInspired by ThinKV's phase-aware perspective on reasoning-time compression~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}, each generated sequence is partitioned into three phases (low-, mid-, high-sparsity) by tercile boundaries of per-position attention sparsity, using a quantile-based segmentation rather than explicit reasoning delimiters (e.g., \\texttt{&lt;think>} tokens). Low-sparsity phases correspond to broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning); high-sparsity phases to concentrated attention (structured computation). We note that this segmentation is an \\emph{operationalization choice}: attention sparsity serves as a continuous proxy for reasoning phase rather than a theoretically grounded boundary. We validate robustness to segmentation granularity (tercile/quartile/quintile; Appendix~\\ref{app:seg-robust}) and minimum phase length (Appendix~\\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}). Validation against DeepSeek's explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} markers using adaptive per-sequence quantile segmentation yields substantial agreement ($\\kappa{=}0.88$, AUC-ROC${=}0.98$; $n{=}48$; Appendix~\\ref{app:think-validation}). Details in Appendix~\\ref{app:formulation-details}.\n",

26	\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification}
27	\label{sec:phase-amplification}
28	
29	Decomposing II by reasoning phase reveals that strategy-dependent severity is driven by amplification in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\ref{tab:main-results} reports the per-phase breakdown. Under importance-based eviction, the median II in the low-sparsity phase rises from $0.357$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $8.456$ ($s{=}0.30$), while the high-sparsity phase remains near zero ($0.003$--$0.038$), yielding paired median differences $\Delta$ from $0.39$ to $8.48$ (all $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction shows a detectable but much weaker phase gradient at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$), $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than importance-based at matched sparsity. Figure~\ref{fig:hero} visualizes the dose-response relationship for both models. The per-problem amplification ratio $G$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) provides a complementary ratio perspective and is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric}.
30	Under the multiplicative null ($r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$; Appendix~\ref{app:multiplicative-null}), the phase-severity gradient is preserved ($G_{\times}{=}21$--$64{\times}$); we report additive-null values throughout as the more conservative estimates.
31	
32	\begin{table*}[t]
33	\centering
34	\small
35	\caption{Per-phase interaction index (II) at $n{=}496$/$475$. $\Delta{=}\widetilde{\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{low}} - \mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{high}}}$: median of per-problem paired differences (not the difference of column medians) with bootstrap 95\% CI ($B{=}10{,}000$). $G$ values reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric}. Profiling-subset values ($n{=}50$) in Table~\ref{tab:profiling-subset}.}
36	\label{tab:main-results}

$[0.61,\\; 1.26]$ & $< 10^{-13}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{@{}p{0.92\\textwidth}}{\\footnotesize $^{\\ddagger}$ Negative II at this sparsity reflects \\texttt{bfloat16} numerical noise when the interaction effect approaches zero ($|\\mathrm{II}| < 10^{-4}$). $\\Delta{=}0.07$, $p{=}8.7 \\times 10^{-15}$ supports $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low}} > \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_ii_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Paired median difference $\\Delta$ (primary metric) as a function of sparsity level $s$ for DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}496$, left) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n{=}475$, right). Importance-based eviction (red) exhibits exponentially increasing $\\Delta$ ($0.39$--$8.48$ for DeepSeek, $0.07$--$3.16$ for Qwen); random eviction (blue) remains near zero at low sparsity (ns) and shows weak effects at $s \\geq 0.25$. Error bars show asymmetric bootstrap 95\\% CIs. DeepSeek importance effects are ${\\sim}2.7{\\times}$ larger than Qwen, consistent with the training-paradigm contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Significance: {*}{*}{*}\\,$p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.001$; {*}{*}\\,$p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.01$; ns\\,$p_{\\text{Bonf}} > 0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:hero}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover}\n\\label{sec:crossover}\n\nThe phase-strategy crossover is model-dependent. For DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at all four sparsity levels: at $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields \\emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\\ $1.113$) but \\emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$), with reversal magnitude ranging from $2.2{\\times}$ to $4.0{\\times}$ across $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. Qwen does \\emph{not} exhibit this crossover: importance-based eviction produces lower II than random in both phases (e.g., at $s{=}0.25$: high $0.004$ vs.\\ $1.108$; low $1.053$ vs.\\ $1.769$). This divergence suggests the crossover arises from model-specific attention head specialization patterns rather than a universal compound-sparsity property. Full crossover analysis appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:crossover-details}.\n\n\\subsection{Replication Across Training Paradigms}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (distillation-trained) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (instruction-tuned) share the Qwen2 backbone but differ in training objective, providing a within-architecture-family comparison across training paradigms.\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\\text{eff}}{=}475$) reproduces the monotonic severity gradient: $\\Delta$ increases from $0.07$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 10^{-14}$), paralleling DeepSeek at approximately one-quarter to one-sixth the magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing median $\\Delta$ trajectories (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z \\geq 6.2$, $p < 10^{-9}$; see \\S\\ref{par:stats}). Random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$, becoming significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($\\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$; $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$) but $2.7$--$20{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based. A caveat: 45.2\\% of Qwen problems yield null mid-phase segments due to low temporal autocorrelation ($r{=}0.718$ vs.\\ $0.894$); this does not affect $\\Delta$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:qwen-null-rate}). Full tables in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-details}.\n\n\\subsection{Preliminary Cross-Architecture Analysis}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, $n{=}49$)\\footnote{The $n{=}49$ sample is an initial profiling subset. Full-scale ($n{=}500$) LlamaForCausalLM replication is planned as future work; the current analysis prioritizes within-architecture replication (Qwen2) over cross-architecture breadth.} provides preliminary support for the phase-gated severity pattern under importance-based eviction (post-hoc power $> 0.99$ for within-importance gradient) but is inconclusive for strategy comparisons (power $0.26$--$0.63$); the interaction magnitude is ${\\sim}40{\\times}$ smaller than DeepSeek, suggesting that quantitative characteristics may be Qwen2-family-specific and that broader architectural diversity (e.g., Mistral, Gemma) is needed for generalization claims. Full results, including per-sparsity breakdowns and a power analysis motivating the sample size needed for definitive cross-architecture claims, appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-arch}.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{sec:cross-benchmark}\n\nTo assess task generalization, we evaluate both models on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) at $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. On DeepSeek (clean data, no output truncation), importance-based eviction replicates the MATH-500 phase gradient across all four sparsity levels with a strict dose-response: $\\Delta{=}0.24$ at $s{=}0.15$ rising to $4.68$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all $p \\leq 0.0003$, Wilcoxon). Strategy specificity on GSM8K manifests as a \\emph{qualitative} gap: importance-based eviction produces significant phase gradients while random eviction is non-significant across all conditions (CIs spanning zero). We attribute this to \\emph{task complexity}: GSM8K's simpler arithmetic is associated with more uniform attention distributions, yielding a flatter sparsity landscape; within MATH-500, longer traces exhibit higher strategy specificity (Spearman $\\rho > 0$, $p < 0.02$), supporting cognitive load as the modulating variable. Qwen GSM8K results are inconclusive due to generation truncation (\\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}${=}$2047): while directional consistency holds at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($\\Delta{=}1.14$--$4.62$; $p < 10^{-4}$), absolute values and phase assignments are unreliable because all outputs hit the token limit. Full tables in Appendix~\\ref{app:gsm8k}.\n\n\\paragraph{GSM8K accuracy under compound sparsity.}\nA power analysis on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) reveals a strategy-dependent asymmetry in downstream accuracy. Under random head removal, all 8 conditions (2 models $\\times$ 4 sparsity levels) are non-significant (max $|d|{=}0.164$). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $n{=}50$ can detect effects of $d \\geq 0.40$ ($\\alpha{=}0.05$, power${=}0.80$); all random conditions showed $|d| \\leq 0.164$, meaning the null results may reflect insufficient power rather than true absence of effect. Under importance-based pruning, Qwen exhibits a medium-sized accuracy degradation at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($d \\approx 0.63$, power ${>}\\,0.99$), consistent with the PPL-based severity gradient observed on MATH-500. DeepSeek importance conditions exhibit a qualitative downward trend in accuracy but are excluded from parametric inference due to heavy-tailed distributions that violate test assumptions.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Validation}\n\\label{sec:stat-validation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports the paired median difference $\\Delta$ under importance-based eviction for both models at $n{=}496$/$475$. All eight conditions yield CIs excluding zero; within-importance dose-response $d_z$ (s015 vs.\\ s030) is $0.94$ (DeepSeek) and $0.92$ (Qwen), both large effects. Importance-based eviction produces significantly larger phase gradients than random at all four sparsity levels ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} \\ll 10^{-10}$, Wilcoxon; permutation tests in Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation-details}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Median of paired differences ($\\Delta_i = \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{low}} - \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{high}}$) under importance-based eviction with bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:cohens-d}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & DS ($n{=}496$): $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$\\; [95\\% CI] & QW ($n{=}475$): $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$\\; [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & $0.39$\\; $[0.35,\\; 0.44]$ & $0.07$\\; $[0.05,\\; 0.09]$ \\\\\n0.20 & $1.21$\\; $[1.11,\\; 1.34]$ & $0.31$\\; $[0.26,\\; 0.36]$ \\\\\n0.25 & $3.99$\\; $[3.53,\\; 4.36]$ & $1.07$\\; $[0.98,\\; 1.18]$ \\\\\n0.30 & $8.48$\\; $[7.75,\\; 9.69]$ & $3.16$\\; $[2.78,\\; 3.56]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize All $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$. Within-importance $d_z$ (s015 vs.\\ s030): DS $0.94$, QW $0.92$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Answer-Region Analysis}\n\\label{sec:answer-region}\n\nAcross both models, 93.2\\% of answer tokens (\\verb|\\boxed{}| markers) fall within the high-sparsity phase, where compound interaction is minimal ($G \\approx 1$). This non-uniformity identifies \\emph{where} severity concentrates: reasoning-trace tokens show $5.7$--$43.5{\\times}$ PPL degradation vs.\\ $1.3$--$5.3{\\times}$ for answer tokens (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). However, reasoning-trace degradation may produce \\emph{silent reasoning errors}---correct formatting with corrupted intermediate logic \\cite{lanham-2023-faithfulness, turpin-2023-biased-cot}---a failure mode that PPL detects but answer-level accuracy may miss.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Accuracy Validation}\n\\label{sec:accuracy-validation}\n\nTo validate that the PPL-based severity gradient translates to downstream task performance, we evaluate MATH-500 accuracy under compound sparsity (importance-based eviction, \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}${=}$8192; Table~\\ref{tab:accuracy}). Qwen's minimal truncation ($\\leq 4\\%$) provides a clean PPL-accuracy bridge.\nQwen ($n{=}100$; truncation $\\leq 4\\%$) shows gradual decline from $46\\%$ (dense) through $s{=}0.25$ ($\\delta{=}-4$ to $-13$\\,pp) with a sharp cliff at $s{=}0.30$ ($19\\%$, $\\delta{=}-27$\\,pp). DeepSeek ($n{=}50$) degrades more steeply ($74\\% \\to 4\\%$) with a dual-regime structure: at $s \\leq 0.20$, degradation is primarily truncation-driven (non-truncated accuracy remains $68$--$71\\%$ at $s{=}0.15$--$0.20$ vs.\\ $88\\%$ dense); at $s \\geq 0.25$, both truncation and reasoning quality collapse simultaneously (Appendix~\\ref{app:accuracy-details}).\nThe two models exhibit distinct failure modes reflecting training-paradigm dependence: DeepSeek failures are \\emph{truncation-dominated} (truncation $16\\% \\to 76\\%$), while Qwen failures are \\emph{wrong-answer-dominated} (truncation $\\leq 4\\%$, reasoning quality degrades directly). Given minimal truncation, Qwen accuracy data provide a reliable PPL-accuracy bridge; DeepSeek accuracy results, confounded by truncation rates of $16\\%$--$76\\%$, should be interpreted as qualitative observations only.\n\nWe provide a correlational mechanistic analysis in terms of importance discrimination collapse in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n",

Error bars show asymmetric bootstrap 95\\% CIs. DeepSeek importance effects are ${\\sim}2.7{\\times}$ larger than Qwen, consistent with the training-paradigm contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Significance: {*}{*}{*}\\,$p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.001$; {*}{*}\\,$p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.01$; ns\\,$p_{\\text{Bonf}} > 0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:hero}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover}\n\\label{sec:crossover}\n\nThe phase-strategy crossover is model-dependent. For DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at all four sparsity levels: at $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields \\emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\\ $1.113$) but \\emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$), with reversal magnitude ranging from $2.2{\\times}$ to $4.0{\\times}$ across $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. Qwen does \\emph{not} exhibit this crossover: importance-based eviction produces lower II than random in both phases (e.g., at $s{=}0.25$: high $0.004$ vs.\\ $1.108$; low $1.053$ vs.\\ $1.769$). This divergence suggests the crossover arises from model-specific attention head specialization patterns rather than a universal compound-sparsity property. Full crossover analysis appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:crossover-details}.\n\n\\subsection{Replication Across Training Paradigms}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (distillation-trained) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (instruction-tuned) share the Qwen2 backbone but differ in training objective, providing a within-architecture-family comparison across training paradigms.\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\\text{eff}}{=}475$) reproduces the monotonic severity gradient: $\\Delta$ increases from $0.07$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 10^{-14}$), paralleling DeepSeek at approximately one-quarter to one-sixth the magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing median $\\Delta$ trajectories (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z \\geq 6.2$, $p < 10^{-9}$; see \\S\\ref{par:stats}). Random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$, becoming significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($\\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$; $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$) but $2.7$--$20{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based. A caveat: 45.2\\% of Qwen problems yield null mid-phase segments due to low temporal autocorrelation ($r{=}0.718$ vs.\\ $0.894$); this does not affect $\\Delta$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:qwen-null-rate}). Full tables in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-details}.\n\n\\subsection{Preliminary Cross-Architecture Analysis}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, $n{=}49$)\\footnote{The $n{=}49$ sample is an initial profiling subset. Full-scale ($n{=}500$) LlamaForCausalLM replication is planned as future work; the current analysis prioritizes within-architecture replication (Qwen2) over cross-architecture breadth.} provides preliminary support for the phase-gated severity pattern under importance-based eviction (post-hoc power $> 0.99$ for within-importance gradient) but is inconclusive for strategy comparisons (power $0.26$--$0.63$); the interaction magnitude is ${\\sim}40{\\times}$ smaller than DeepSeek, suggesting that quantitative characteristics may be Qwen2-family-specific and that broader architectural diversity (e.g., Mistral, Gemma) is needed for generalization claims. Full results, including per-sparsity breakdowns and a power analysis motivating the sample size needed for definitive cross-architecture claims, appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-arch}.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{sec:cross-benchmark}\n\nTo assess task generalization, we evaluate both models on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) at $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. On DeepSeek (clean data, no output truncation), importance-based eviction replicates the MATH-500 phase gradient across all four sparsity levels with a strict dose-response: $\\Delta{=}0.24$ at $s{=}0.15$ rising to $4.68$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all $p \\leq 0.0003$, Wilcoxon). Strategy specificity on GSM8K manifests as a \\emph{qualitative} gap: importance-based eviction produces significant phase gradients while random eviction is non-significant across all conditions (CIs spanning zero). We attribute this to \\emph{task complexity}: GSM8K's simpler arithmetic is associated with more uniform attention distributions, yielding a flatter sparsity landscape; within MATH-500, longer traces exhibit higher strategy specificity (Spearman $\\rho > 0$, $p < 0.02$), supporting cognitive load as the modulating variable. Qwen GSM8K results are inconclusive due to generation truncation (\\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}${=}$2047): while directional consistency holds at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($\\Delta{=}1.14$--$4.62$; $p < 10^{-4}$), absolute values and phase assignments are unreliable because all outputs hit the token limit. Full tables in Appendix~\\ref{app:gsm8k}.\n\n\\paragraph{GSM8K accuracy under compound sparsity.}\nA power analysis on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) reveals a strategy-dependent asymmetry in downstream accuracy. Under random head removal, all 8 conditions (2 models $\\times$ 4 sparsity levels) are non-significant (max $|d|{=}0.164$). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $n{=}50$ can detect effects of $d \\geq 0.40$ ($\\alpha{=}0.05$, power${=}0.80$); all random conditions showed $|d| \\leq 0.164$, meaning the null results may reflect insufficient power rather than true absence of effect. Under importance-based pruning, Qwen exhibits a medium-sized accuracy degradation at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($d \\approx 0.63$, power ${>}\\,0.99$), consistent with the PPL-based severity gradient observed on MATH-500. DeepSeek importance conditions exhibit a qualitative downward trend in accuracy but are excluded from parametric inference due to heavy-tailed distributions that violate test assumptions.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Validation}\n\\label{sec:stat-validation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports the paired median difference $\\Delta$ under importance-based eviction for both models at $n{=}496$/$475$. All eight conditions yield CIs excluding zero; within-importance dose-response $d_z$ (s015 vs.\\ s030) is $0.94$ (DeepSeek) and $0.92$ (Qwen), both large effects. Importance-based eviction produces significantly larger phase gradients than random at all four sparsity levels ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} \\ll 10^{-10}$, Wilcoxon; permutation tests in Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation-details}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Median of paired differences ($\\Delta_i = \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{low}} - \\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\textit{high}}$) under importance-based eviction with bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:cohens-d}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & DS ($n{=}496$): $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$\\; [95\\% CI] & QW ($n{=}475$): $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$\\; [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & $0.39$\\; $[0.35,\\; 0.44]$ & $0.07$\\; $[0.05,\\; 0.09]$ \\\\\n0.20 & $1.21$\\; $[1.11,\\; 1.34]$ & $0.31$\\; $[0.26,\\; 0.36]$ \\\\\n0.25 & $3.99$\\; $[3.53,\\; 4.36]$ & $1.07$\\; $[0.98,\\; 1.18]$ \\\\\n0.30 & $8.48$\\; $[7.75,\\; 9.69]$ & $3.16$\\; $[2.78,\\; 3.56]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize All $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$. Within-importance $d_z$ (s015 vs.\\ s030): DS $0.94$, QW $0.92$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Answer-Region Analysis}\n\\label{sec:answer-region}\n\nAcross both models, 93.2\\% of answer tokens (\\verb|\\boxed{}| markers) fall within the high-sparsity phase, where compound interaction is minimal ($G \\approx 1$). This non-uniformity identifies \\emph{where} severity concentrates: reasoning-trace tokens show $5.7$--$43.5{\\times}$ PPL degradation vs.\\ $1.3$--$5.3{\\times}$ for answer tokens (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). However, reasoning-trace degradation may produce \\emph{silent reasoning errors}---correct formatting with corrupted intermediate logic \\cite{lanham-2023-faithfulness, turpin-2023-biased-cot}---a failure mode that PPL detects but answer-level accuracy may miss.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Accuracy Validation}\n\\label{sec:accuracy-validation}\n\nTo validate that the PPL-based severity gradient translates to downstream task performance, we evaluate MATH-500 accuracy under compound sparsity (importance-based eviction, \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}${=}$8192; Table~\\ref{tab:accuracy}). Qwen's minimal truncation ($\\leq 4\\%$) provides a clean PPL-accuracy bridge.\nQwen ($n{=}100$; truncation $\\leq 4\\%$) shows gradual decline from $46\\%$ (dense) through $s{=}0.25$ ($\\delta{=}-4$ to $-13$\\,pp) with a sharp cliff at $s{=}0.30$ ($19\\%$, $\\delta{=}-27$\\,pp). DeepSeek ($n{=}50$) degrades more steeply ($74\\% \\to 4\\%$) with a dual-regime structure: at $s \\leq 0.20$, degradation is primarily truncation-driven (non-truncated accuracy remains $68$--$71\\%$ at $s{=}0.15$--$0.20$ vs.\\ $88\\%$ dense); at $s \\geq 0.25$, both truncation and reasoning quality collapse simultaneously (Appendix~\\ref{app:accuracy-details}).\nThe two models exhibit distinct failure modes reflecting training-paradigm dependence: DeepSeek failures are \\emph{truncation-dominated} (truncation $16\\% \\to 76\\%$), while Qwen failures are \\emph{wrong-answer-dominated} (truncation $\\leq 4\\%$, reasoning quality degrades directly). Given minimal truncation, Qwen accuracy data provide a reliable PPL-accuracy bridge; DeepSeek accuracy results, confounded by truncation rates of $16\\%$--$76\\%$, should be interpreted as qualitative observations only.\n\nWe provide a correlational mechanistic analysis in terms of importance discrimination collapse in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n",
